P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}=\Pi(z)\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\rho\sigma}\quad M_{\alpha\rho}=0\quad T_{\alpha\beta\rho}=0\,.

(131)

We have thus assumed that the replicated bosonic fields decouple from the replicated soft spins, and that there are no correlations among bosonic fields in different replicas. Using this structure, the saddle point equations for the overlap matrix
Q_{\alpha\beta}
and the magnetization
m_{\alpha}
become

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}=\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\quad m_{\alpha}=\langle x^{\alpha}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\,

(132)

while the saddle point equation for
\Pi(z)
reads

\Pi(z)=\left\langle\frac{1}{z-E_{I}^{\prime\prime}(x^{\alpha},a)-\Pi(z)}\right\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\,.

(133)

The mean field average
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
is an average over the replicated soft spin variables:

\langle\ldots\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\equiv\frac{1}{Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\{x^{\alpha}\}]}\,,

(134)

and the mean field energy is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=\sum_{\alpha}E_{I}(x^{\alpha})-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\\
&-J_{0}\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\\
Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\{x^{\alpha}\}]}\,.\end{split}

(135)

We now have all the elements to compute the resolvent in Eq. (
25
). Taking the derivative with respect to
z
of the action we obtain

\begin{split}G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)&=-\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{l\to 0}\frac{i}{n}\partial_{l}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\rho,\sigma}\langle\phi^{\alpha\rho}\phi^{\beta\sigma}\rangle_{x,\phi}\\
&=\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{l\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\partial_{l}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\rho,\sigma}P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\\
&=\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{l\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\partial_{l}nl\Pi(z)\\
&=\lim_{n\to 0}\Pi(z)\,.\end{split}

(136)

In the second line, we used the saddle point equation in Eq. (
129
), and in the third line we used our ansatz for the order parameter
P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}
given by Eq. (
131
). The resolvent can be thus identified with the quantity
\Pi(z)
. This implies that the resolvent satisfies a self-consistent equation

G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)=\lim_{n\to 0}\left\langle\frac{1}{z-\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)}\right\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\,,

(137)

which can be rewritten, using the Dirac delta distribution, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)&=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\int\text{d}x\,\frac{\langle\delta(x-x^{\alpha})\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{z-\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x)-G(z)}\\
&=\int\text{d}x\,\frac{P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)}{z-\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x,a)-G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)}\,.\end{split}

(138)

The second line defines the single spin probability distribution
P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)
as

P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\langle\delta(x-x^{\alpha})\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\,.

(139)

Equation (
138
) corresponds to Eq. (
26
) in the main text.

B.1
Stability of the ansatz of Eq. (
131
)

The ansatz chosen in Eq. (
131
) is valid as long as the action
S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\bm{\Gamma},z]
has a maximum at
\bm{\Gamma}=\bm{\Gamma}^{*}
, with
\bm{\Gamma}^{*}
the set of order parameters satisfying the saddle-point equations, Eq. (
131
) and Eq. (
133
). To check this condition, we expand the action around its saddle-point value:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\bm{\Gamma}^{*}&+\delta\bm{\Gamma},\,z]\approx S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\bm{\Gamma}^{*},\,z]\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\bm{\Gamma},\,z]}{\partial\Gamma_{\nu}\partial\Gamma_{\nu^{\prime}}}\Biggr\rvert_{\bm{\Gamma}=\bm{\Gamma}^{*}}\delta\Gamma_{\nu}\delta\Gamma_{\nu^{\prime}}\,.\end{split}

(140)

The set of parameters
\bm{\Gamma}^{*}
maximizes the action as long as the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}[\bm{\Gamma},\,z]}{\partial\Gamma_{\nu}\partial\Gamma_{\nu^{\prime}}}\Biggr\rvert_{\bm{\Gamma}=\bm{\Gamma}^{*}}
are all negative. Since within our ansatz in Eq. (
131
) the bosonic fields and the soft spin variables are decoupled, we restrict our study on the subspace spanned the order parameters
P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma},\,T_{\alpha\beta\rho},\,M_{\alpha\rho}
. Within our ansatz, the only non zero entries of the Hessian matrix in Eq. (
140
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\partial P_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}}&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}+\delta_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\rho\sigma^{\prime}}\delta_{\sigma\rho^{\prime}}\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\left[\langle\phi^{\alpha\rho}\phi^{\beta\sigma}\phi^{\alpha^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}\phi^{\beta^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}-\langle\phi^{\alpha\rho}\phi^{\beta\sigma}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\langle\phi^{\alpha^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}\phi^{\beta^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\right]\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial T_{\alpha\beta\rho}\partial T_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}}&=i\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}-\beta^{2}\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\alpha^{\prime}}\phi^{\beta\rho}\phi^{\beta\rho^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}=i\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}-\beta^{2}Q_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\phi^{\beta\rho}\phi^{\beta^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial M_{\alpha\rho}\partial M_{\alpha^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}}&=i\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}-J_{0}\langle\phi^{\alpha\rho}\phi^{\alpha^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\,.\end{split}

(141)

The real parts of the terms involving partial derivatives with respect to
T_{\alpha\beta\rho}
or
M_{\alpha\rho}
are negative, and fluctuations within these subspace are thus irrelevant. We focus therefore on the block of the Hessian involving fluctuations of the tensor
P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}
. The only nonzero entries in this block are

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\partial P_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}}=-\frac{1}{2}\left[1+\langle\left(\phi^{\alpha\rho}\right)\rangle^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}\right]^{2}\\
&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial P_{\alpha\alpha\rho\rho}\partial P_{\alpha\alpha\rho\rho}}=-1\\
&+\int\text{d}x\,\frac{P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\left[z-\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x,a)-G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(z)\right]^{2}}=0\,,\end{split}

(142)

where the
\alpha\neq\beta
and
\rho\neq\sigma
. The term on the first line of Eq. (
142
) has a negative definite real part, while the term on the second line is zero, as it can be verified by taking the derivative of the saddle point equation for the resolvent in Eq. (
137
) with respect to
G^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
. Our ansatz is therefore marginally stable against longitudinal fluctuations in the space of the tensor
P_{\alpha\alpha\rho\rho}
. Motivated by past work in the analysis of the ground states of disordered systems
Franz et al. 2015
, we assume that no instability develops when considering perturbations of higher order.

Appendix C
Replica symmetric ansatz

In this Section we show how to obtain the saddle point equations in the replica symmetric case, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\alpha\beta}&=q_{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}+q_{o}(1-\delta_{\alpha\beta})\\
m_{\alpha}&=m\,.\end{split}

(143)

Plugging Eq. (
143
) into the mean-field partition function given by Eq. (
135
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\,\exp\Biggl[-\beta\sum_{\alpha}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)\\
&-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\Delta q\sum_{\alpha}(x^{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}q_{o}\left(\sum_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\right)^{2}\\
&+J_{0}m\sum_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\Biggr]\end{split}

(144)

We now use the rescaled overlap gap
\Delta\widetilde{q}\equiv\beta(q_{d}-q_{o})
and perform a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation, obtaining

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\,\overline{\exp\Biggl[-\beta\sum_{\alpha}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)}\\
&\overline{-\frac{\beta}{2}\Delta\widetilde{q}\sum_{\alpha}(x^{\alpha})^{2}-\beta(\sqrt{q_{o}}h-J_{0}m)x^{\alpha}\Biggr]}\\
&\equiv\overline{\left(\int\text{d}x\,\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x,h)}\right)^{n}}\,\\
&\equiv\overline{\left(Z_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(h)\right)^{n}}\,,\end{split}

(145)

where
\overline{\ldots}\equiv\int\frac{\text{d}h}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\ldots\text{e}^{-h^{2}/2}
is an average over the realization of a random Gaussian field
h
, which acts as a quenched disorder in the system. In the second line, we have observed that the partition function factorizes among different replicas and we defined the replica symmetric energy function

E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x,h)\equiv E_{I}(x,a+\Delta\widetilde{q})-(\sqrt{q_{o}}h+J_{0}m)x\,.

(146)

The last line of Eq. (
145
) defines the replica symmetric partition function
Z_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(h)
for a given realization of the Gaussian field
h
. We compute in a similar fashion the single spin distribution in Eq. (
139
) within the replica symmetric phase,

\displaystyle\begin{split}P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)&=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\int\prod_{\beta=1}^{n}\text{d}x^{\beta}\,\delta(x-x^{\alpha})\\
&\times\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}[\{x^{\beta}\}]}\left[Z^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}\right]^{-1}\\
&=\lim_{n\to 0}\overline{\text{e}^{-\beta E{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x,z)}\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}(h)\right)^{n-1}}\\
&=\overline{\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}(x,z)}\left[Z^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}(h)\right]^{-1}}\\
&\equiv P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}(x)\,,\end{split}

(147)

which is Eq. (
38
) of the main text.

Appendix D
Stability of the replica symmetric solution

The replica symmetric solution is stable as long as the order parameters that satisfy the saddle point equations in Eq. (
36
) are an extremum of the action
S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
. This requirement breaks down when the Hessian of the action, introduced in Eq. (
140
), acquires a positive eigenvalue along some direction related to a perturbation of the overlap matrix
Q_{\alpha\beta}
and to the magnetization
m_{\alpha}
. The Hessian of the action involves the following components,

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\gamma\epsilon}}=-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\gamma\epsilon}+\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\epsilon})\\
&+\frac{\beta^{4}}{2}\left(\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}x^{\gamma}x^{\epsilon}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}-\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\,\overline{\langle x^{\gamma}x^{\epsilon}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\right)\\
\textasciibreve&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial m_{\gamma}}=\beta^{3}J_{0}\left[\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}x^{\gamma}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}-\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\,\overline{\langle x^{\gamma}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\right]\\
&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}\partial m_{\beta}}=-\beta J_{0}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
&+(\beta J_{0})^{2}\left[\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}-\overline{\langle x^{\alpha}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\,\overline{\langle x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(148)

The first eigenvalue that changes sign at the instability transition is known as the replicon,
\Lambda_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
. Its expression depends on the generic structure of the overlap matrix
Q_{\alpha\beta}
, rather than the specific model at hand, and it is given by
Sherrington 1978
;
De Dominicis and Giardina 2006

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=2\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}}-4\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\beta\gamma}}+2\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\gamma\epsilon}}\\
&=\beta^{2}\left[-1+\beta^{2}\overline{\left(\left\langle x^{2}\right\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}-\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}{}^{2}\right)^{2}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(149)

The replica-symmetric saddle point is stable as long as
\Lambda_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}<0
. In the low temperature limit
\beta\to\infty
, it is convenient to consider a rescaled version of the replicon eigenvalue
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\equiv\beta^{-2}\Lambda_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
. A saddle point evaluation of the replica-symmetric average in Eq. (
149
) yields the following stability condition for the replica symmetric phase,

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}=-1+\overline{[\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h))]^{-2}}<0\,,

(150)

where
x^{*}(h)
is the minimum of the replica symmetric mean field energy
E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}
in Eq. (
146
) for a fixed realization of the quenched field
h
. We thus recover Eq. (
42
) of the main text.

Appendix E
Derivation of Eq. (
44
)

In this Appendix we compute the stability boundary of the convex paramagnetic phase for the CIM-SK. Starting from the replica-symmetric saddle point equations Eq. (
36
), we consider the case where
m=q_{o}=0
. The only relevant order parameter is
\Delta\widetilde{q}
, which can be computed from the saddle point equations as

\Delta\widetilde{q}=-\frac{1}{a+\Delta\widetilde{q}}\,.

(151)

The stability of this paramagnetic phase is determined by the condition
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}<0
in Eq. (
42
), which for
m=0,\,q_{o}=0
yields

\Delta\widetilde{q}^{2}<1\implies\Delta\widetilde{q}<1\,.

(152)

We also need to impose the paramagnetic state to be stable against perturbation of the magnetization, i.e. that
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}\partial m_{\beta}}<0
. From Eq. (
148
) we obtain

\Delta\widetilde{q}<J_{0}^{-1}\,.

(153)

Combining Eq. (
152
) and Eq. (
153
) we obtain

\Delta\widetilde{q}<\min[1,\,J_{0}^{-1}]\,.

(154)

This is Eq. (
44
) of the main text.

Appendix F
Outline of the derivation of the full replica symmetry breaking equations

In this Appendix we outline how to derive the full replica symmetry breaking equation for the CIM-SK, presented in Sec.
VI.2
. When the replica-symmetric solution becomes unstable, a new ansatz for the overlap matrix
Q
is required. Different replicas are no longer equivalent, and their overlaps organize in a hierarchical structure
Parisi 1979
;
Parisi 1980
;
Mézard et al. 1987
;
Nishimori 2001
. All the rows of
Q
are the same up to a permutation of their elements. The description of the first row of
Q
is thus enough to characterize the overlap matrix. The first row is divided in
k+2
bands of different lengths. We label each band by an index
i=0,\ldots,k+1
. The band with index
k+1
corresponds to the first diagonal entry of the matrix
Q
. For
i\leq k+1
, the
i
th band contains
m_{i}-m_{i+1}
elements, all equal to a given overlap value
q_{i}
. The set
\{m_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k+1}
is ordered in the following way

1=m_{k+1}<m_{k}<\ldots<m_{1}<m_{0}=n\,.

(155)

The overlap matrix becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\alpha\beta}&=q_{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
&+(1-\delta_{\alpha\beta})\left[q_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(q_{i}-q_{i-1})I_{\alpha\beta}^{m_{i}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(156)

where
I_{\alpha\beta}^{m_{i}}
is an
n\times n
covered along its diagonal by
n/m_{i}
block matrices of size
m_{i}\times m_{i}
, with all the entries equal to
1
. When the limit
n\to 0
is taken by means of an analytic continuation, the order of the size of the bands is inverted, so that

0=m_{0}<m_{1}<\ldots<m_{k}<m_{k+1}=1\,.

(157)

Using this structure, the mean field partition function
Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
becomes
Nishimori 2001

\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}=\beta\int\mathcal{D}h_{0}\,f_{1}(h_{0}\sqrt{q_{0}})\,,

(158)

where
\mathcal{D}h_{0}\equiv\frac{\text{d}h_{0}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\text{e}^{-\frac{h_{0}^{2}}{2}}
is the Gaussian measure. The function
f_{1}(h)
is the first member of a hierarchy of
k+1
’free energies’, which satisfy a recursion relation,

f_{i}(h)=\frac{1}{\beta m_{i}}\log\int\mathcal{D}h_{i}\,\text{e}^{\beta m_{i}f_{i+1}(h+\sqrt{q_{i}-q_{i-1}}z_{i})}\,,

(159)

together with the boundary condition

f_{k+1}(h)=\frac{1}{\beta}\log\int\text{d}x\,\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}(x,h)}\,,

(160)

from which we recognize
f_{k+1}
as the free energy of a single soft spin system with energy
E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}
. The field
h
acts as a form of quenched disorder, but its distribution is no longer Gaussian, because of the hierarchical structure of the states of the system.

The saddle point equations for the overlaps
q_{i},\,q_{d}
and the magnetization
m
can be computed as derivatives of the logarithm of the partition function in Eq. (
158
). We obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}q_{i}&=\int\text{d}hP_{i}(h)\left[\partial_{h}f_{i}(h)\right]^{2}\\
\Delta\widetilde{q}&=\int\text{d}h\,P_{k}(h)[\partial^{2}_{h}f_{k+1}(h)]\\
m&=\int\text{d}h\,P_{k}(h)\partial_{h}f_{k+1}(h)\,.\end{split}

(161)

The function
P_{i}(h)
is the distribution of internal fields
h
acting at the level
i
of the hierarchy. It is defined as the integrated response of the function
f_{1}
to a change of the free energy at the level
i+1
in the hierarchy,

P_{i}(h)\equiv\int\mathcal{D}h_{0}\frac{\delta f_{1}(h_{0}\sqrt{q_{0}})}{\delta f_{i+1}(h)}\,,

(162)

where
\frac{\delta f_{1}(h_{0})}{\delta f_{i+1}(h)}
is a functional derivative. The distribution of internal fields satisfies a recursive relation too, namely,

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{i}(h)&=\int\mathcal{D}h_{i}P_{i-1}(h-\sqrt{q_{i}-q_{i-1}}h_{i})\\
&\times\text{e}^{\beta m_{i}\left(f_{i+1}(h)-f_{i}(h-\sqrt{q_{i}-q_{i-1}}h_{i})\right)}\,,\end{split}

(163)

with the boundary condition

P_{0}(h)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi q_{0}}}\text{e}^{-\frac{h^{2}}{2q_{0}}}\,.

(164)

The single-spin distribution
P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
becomes instead

P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)=\int\text{d}hP_{k}(h)\frac{\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)}}{Z^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(h)}\,.

(165)

The full replica symmetry breaking is obtained when the number of levels
k
in the hierarchy is sent to infinity. The bands in the hierarchical matrix
Q
form then a continuum, and the overlaps are described by a continuous function
q(y)
, with
y\in[0,1]
, which for finite
k
is defined as

q(y)=q_{i}\qquad m_{i}\leq y<m_{i+1}\,.

(166)

The same continuum limit is taken for the hierarchical free energies
f_{i}(h)
and for the internal field distribution
P_{i}(h)
, which become continuous functions
f(y,h)
and
P(y,h)
respectively. The recursive relations given by Eq. (
159
) and Eq. (
163
) become a backward and a forward Fokker-Planck equations, respectively,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{f}(y,h)&=-\frac{\dot{q}(y)}{2}\left[f^{\prime\prime}+\beta y(f^{\prime})^{2}\right]\\
\dot{P}(y,h)&=\frac{\dot{q}(y)}{2}\left[P^{\prime\prime}-2\beta y(Pf^{\prime})^{\prime}\right]\,,\end{split}

(167)

where we used a shorthanded notation for the derivatives,
\dot{f}(y,h)=\partial_{y}f(y,h)
and
f^{\prime}(y,h)=\partial_{h}f(y,h)
. The boundary conditions of the partial differential equations in Eq. (
167
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(y=1,h)&=\frac{1}{\beta}\log\int\text{d}x\,\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x,h)}\\
P(y=0,h)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi q(0)}}\text{e}^{-\frac{h^{2}}{2q(0)}}\,.\end{split}

(168)

The saddle point equations in the continuum limit are

\displaystyle\begin{split}q(y)&=\int\text{d}h\,P(y,h)[f^{\prime}(y,h)]^{2}\\
\Delta\widetilde{q}&=\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)f^{\prime\prime}(1,h)\\
m&=\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)f^{\prime}(1,h)\,,\end{split}

(169)

and the single spin distribution is

P_{\text{\tiny{fRSB}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)=\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)\frac{\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)}}{Z^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(h)}\,.

(170)

F.1
Marginal stability

The replicon in the full replica symmetry breaking phase reads, from its expression in Eq. (
149
),

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}=-1+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,P(1,h)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h)]^{2}\,.

(171)

We now show that
\tilde{\Lambda}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}=0
in the spin-glass phase. Taking the derivative with respect to
y
in the saddle point equation for
q(y)
Eq. (
169
), yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{q}(y)&=\int\text{d}h\dot{P}(y,h)[f^{\prime}(y,h)]^{2}\\
&+2\int\text{d}h\,P(y,h)\dot{f}^{\prime}(y,h)f^{\prime}(y,h)\,.\end{split}

(172)

Substituting Eq. (
167
) into the expression above and integrating by parts several times we obtain

\dot{q}(y)=\dot{q}(y)\int\text{d}h\,P(y,h)[f^{\prime\prime}(y,h)]^{2}\,,

(173)

which implies, for
y=1
,

-1+\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h)]^{2}=0\,.

(174)

From the expression of the replicon in Eq. (
171
) we thus conclude that

\tilde{\Lambda}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}=0

(175)

in the spin-glass phase.

F.2
Low temperature solution

In the low temperature limit
\beta\to\infty
the boundary condition for the function
f(y,h)
is evaluated through a saddle point method, yielding

f(y=1,h)=-E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h),h)\,,

(176)

where
x^{*}(h)
is the minimizer of the energy
E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
for a given realization of the internal field
h
. This minimizer satisfies the equation

x^{*}{}(h)^{3}-(a+\Delta\widetilde{q})x^{*}(h)-J_{0}m=h\,.

(177)

If
a+\Delta\widetilde{q}\leq 0
, then Eq. (
177
) has a unique solution. The higher order derivatives of
f(y=1,h)
can be obtained from Eq. (
176
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{\prime}(y=1,h)&=x^{*}(h)\\
f^{\prime\prime}(y=1,h)&=\partial_{h}x^{*}(h)=\frac{1}{3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q}}\,.\end{split}

(178)

The saddle point equations Eq. (
169
) then become

\displaystyle\begin{split}q(y)&=\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)[x^{*}(h)]^{2}\\
\Delta\widetilde{q}&=\int\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q}}\\
m&=\int\text{d}h\,P(1,h)x^{*}(h)\,,\end{split}

(179)

while the marginal stability condition is

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}=-1+\int\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{(3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}=0\,.

(180)

Finally, a saddle-point evaluation of the single spin probability distribution in Eq. (
170
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{\text{\tiny{fRSB}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)&\propto\lvert 3x^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q}\rvert\\
&\times P(1,h=x^{3}-(a+\Delta\widetilde{q})x+J_{0}m)\,.\end{split}

(181)

For
a+\Delta\widetilde{q}>0
, the effective energy
E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}
is nonconvex. Equation (
176
) is still valid, provided that
x^{*}(h)
is the global minimum of
E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
. However, the global minimum
x^{*}(h)
exhibits a discontinuous jump at
h=-J_{0}m
. The integral in marginality condition given by Eq. (
175
) must be carefully handled around the discontinuity as the zero-temperature limit is taken. As discussed in
Folena and Urbani 2022
for a related model of mean -field disordered systems, let us consider two distinct regimes, depending on the strength of the quenched disorder
h
with respect to the temperature
T
. A first regime is obtained when
h+J_{0}m\gg T
, far away from the discontinuity, while a second one is given by
h+J_{0}m\sim T
.The first derivative of the free energy in Eq. (
176
) is split into the two regimes as

f^{\prime}(y=1,h)=\begin{cases}x^{*}(h)\quad\text{if }|h+J_{0}m|\gg T\\
x^{*}(h)\tanh(\beta(h+J_{0}m)x^{*}(h))\quad\text{if }|h+J_{0}m|\sim T\,,\end{cases}

(182)

The integral in the marginal stability condition in Eq. (
175
) is then evaluated in the limit
T\to 0
by separately considering the two regimes. The computation reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,P(1,h)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h)]^{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,P(1,h-J_{0}m)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h-J_{0}m)]^{2}\\
&=\int_{h\in(-\infty,\,-T)\cup(T,\infty)}\text{d}h\,P(1,h-J_{0}m)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h-J_{0}m)]^{2}+\int_{-T}^{T}\text{d}h\,P(1,h-J_{0}m)[f^{\prime\prime}(1,h-J_{0}m)]^{2}\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h))^{2}}+\int_{-T}^{T}\text{d}h\,\beta^{2}P(1,h-J_{0}m)[x^{*}(h-J_{0}m)]^{4}(1-\tanh(\beta h)^{2})^{2}\\
&+\int_{-T}^{T}\text{d}h\,P(1,h-J_{0}m)\frac{[x^{*}(h-J_{0}m)]^{2}(1-\tanh(\beta h)^{2})^{2}(\beta h)^{2}}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h-J_{0}m))}\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h))^{2}}+\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,\beta P(1,T\tilde{h}-J_{0}m)[x^{*}(T\tilde{h}-J_{0}m)]^{4}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}\\
&+\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,TP(1,T\tilde{h})\frac{[x^{*}(T\tilde{h}-J_{0}m)]^{2}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}\tilde{h}^{2}}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(T\tilde{h}-J_{0}m))}\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(h))^{2}}+\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,\tilde{P}(1,\tilde{h})[x^{*}(J_{0}m)]^{4}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}\\
&+T^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,\tilde{P}(1,\tilde{h})\frac{[x^{*}(J_{0}m)]^{2}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}\tilde{h}^{2}}{\partial^{2}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{*}(J_{0}m))}\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{(3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}+\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,\tilde{P}(1,\tilde{h})(a+\Delta\widetilde{q})^{4}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}\end{split}

(183)

In the first equality, we shifted the integration variable by
J_{0}m
. In the second equality, we split the integration domain into two different regions, isolating a region around the origin of width
\sim T
. In the third equality, we explicitly computed the quantity
f^{\prime\prime}(y=1,h)
. In the fourth equality, a rescaled field was introduced
T\tilde{h}\equiv h
. In the fifth equality, we introduced a rescaled and shifted distribution of fields
T\tilde{P}(1,\tilde{h})=P(1,Th+J_{0}m)
. Finally, in the last equality, we substituted for the explicit expression of
x^{*}(h)
and its derivatives. The marginal stability condition in Eq. (
175
) thus reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&=-1+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{(3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}+\int_{-1}^{1}\text{d}\tilde{h}\,\tilde{P}(1,\tilde{h})(a+\Delta\widetilde{q})^{4}(1-\tanh(\tilde{h})^{2})^{2}=0\,.\end{split}

(184)

Notice that the third contribution to the replicon is positive. In order for the marginal stability condition of the full-replica symmetry breaking solution with respect to perturbations in the space of overlap matrices to be satisfied, the internal field distribution must decay to zero around
J_{0}m
at least as fast as linearly, i.e.
P(1,h-J_{0}m)\sim|h|
for
h\ll 1
. Moreover, when
a+\Delta\widetilde{q}>0
the marginality condition implies

-1+\int\text{d}h\,\frac{P(1,h)}{(3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}<0\,.

(185)

This inequality can equivalently be expressed through a change of variable from the internal field distribution to the soft spin distribution as

-1+\int\text{d}x\,\frac{P_{\text{\tiny{fRSB}}}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(x)}{(3x^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}<0\,,

(186)

which is Eq. (
58
) in the main text.

F.3
Numerical solution of full replica symmetry breaking equations

To solve the full replica-symmetry breaking equations at zero temperature, we adopt a method similar to the one developed in
Schmidt and Oppermann 2008
. We discretize the continuous solution into
k
steps. Since we are interested in the low temperature limit of the full replica-symmetry breaking equations, we adopt a rescaled size of the blocks
\widetilde{m}_{i}\equiv\beta m_{i}
, for
i=0,\ldots,k+1
. The rescaled variables
\widetilde{m}_{i}
are ordered in the following way, which can be read from Eq. (
157
),

0<\widetilde{m}_{0}<\widetilde{m}_{1}<\ldots<\widetilde{m}_{k}<\widetilde{m}_{k+1}=\beta\to\infty\,.

(187)

Starting from an initial guess of the discretized Parisi function
q_{i}
, we iteratively solve the recursion relation for the functions
\tilde{f}_{i}(h)\equiv\widetilde{m}_{i}f(h)
,

\tilde{f}_{i}(h)=\log\int\mathcal{D}w_{i}\,\text{e}^{\frac{\widetilde{m}_{i}}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}}\tilde{f}_{i+1}(h+w_{i})}\,,

(188)

where
\mathcal{D}w_{i}\equiv\frac{\text{d}w_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi(q_{i}-q_{i-1})}}\text{e}^{-\frac{w_{i}^{2}}{2(q_{i}-q_{i-1})}}
is a Gaussian measure of variance
q_{i}-q_{i-1}
. The boundary condition of the recursion relation is

\tilde{f}_{k+1}(h)=-\widetilde{m}_{k+1}\min_{x}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)\,.

(189)

Note that the use of the rescaled variable
\tilde{f}_{i}
requires the computation of the ratio
\widetilde{m}_{i}/\widetilde{m}_{i+1}
, which is typically more well-behaved than the quantities
\widetilde{m}_{i}
. We also compute the derivatives
\tilde{f}_{i}^{\prime}(h)
, using a recursion relation obtained from Eq. (
188
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{f}_{i}^{\prime}(h)&=\frac{\widetilde{m}_{i}}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}}\int\mathcal{D}w_{i}\,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}(h+w_{i})\\
&\times\text{e}^{\frac{\tilde{m_{i}}}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}}\tilde{f}_{i+1}(h+w_{i})-\tilde{f}_{i}(h)}\,,\end{split}

(190)

together with the boundary condition

\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{k+1}(h)=\widetilde{m}_{k+1}x^{*}(h)\,,

(191)

with
x^{*}(h)\equiv\argmin_{x}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
. We then compute the probability distributions of the internal fields
P_{i}(h)
using the recursion relations

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{i}(h)&=\int\mathcal{D}w_{i}\,P_{i-1}(h-w_{i})\\
&\times\text{e}^{\frac{\widetilde{m}_{i}}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}}\tilde{f}_{i+1}(h)-\tilde{f}_{i}(h-w_{i})}\,,\end{split}

(192)

with the boundary condition given by Eq. (
164
). If
q_{0}<0.01
, we replace the function
P_{0}
by a Dirac delta centered at the origin. Once the distribution of internal fields are known, we compute the order parameters from the low temperature limit of the saddle point equations Eq. (
161
), which read

\displaystyle\begin{split}q_{i}&=\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2}}\int\text{d}h\,P_{i}(h)[f^{\prime}_{i}(h)]^{2}\\
\Delta\widetilde{q}&=\int\text{d}h\,\frac{P_{k}(h)}{3x^{*}(h)^{2}-a-\Delta\widetilde{q}}\\
m&=\int\text{d}h\,P_{k}(h)x^{*}(h)\,.\end{split}

(193)

The position of the bands
\widetilde{m}_{i}
is updated too, using the formula
Schmidt and Oppermann 2008

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{m}_{i}&=\frac{-B_{i}-\sqrt{B_{i}^{2}-4A_{i}C_{i}}}{2A_{i}}\\
A_{i}&=\frac{1}{4}(q_{i}^{2}-q_{i-1}^{2})\\
B_{i}&=-\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}(1-\delta_{ik})+\delta_{ik}}\int\text{d}h\,P_{i-1}(h)\\
&\times\int\mathcal{D}w_{i}\tilde{f}_{i}(h-w_{i})\text{e}^{\frac{\widetilde{m}_{i}}{\widetilde{m}_{i+1}}\tilde{f}_{i+1}(h-w_{i})-\tilde{f}_{i}(h-w_{i})}\\
C_{i}&=\int\text{d}h\,P_{i-1}(h)\tilde{f}_{i}(h)\,,\end{split}

(194)

with the boundaries
\widetilde{m}_{0}=0
and
\widetilde{m}_{k+1}\gg 1
held fixed. The steps described can be iterated until the desired level of convergence is achieved. To build Fig.
2
(a), we iterated the numerical scheme
20
times with
k=25
, which is enough to obtain convergence of the quantities
m
and
a+\Delta\widetilde{q}
with a degree of accuracy of
\sim 1\%
. At large values of the laser gain, the initial condition for the order parameters
q_{i},\,\Delta\widetilde{q},m
was taken from results obtained at slightly larger laser gains. To obtain the curves for
P^{\text{\tiny SK}}(x)
,
\rho^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\lambda)
and
\lambda_{\text{\tiny m}}(a,J_{0})
in Fig.
2
(b-e) and Fig.
3
the number of iterations was increased to
50
, and the number of steps to
k=35
. Moreover, instead of updating the quantity
\Delta\widetilde{q}
during each iteration, we instead fixed a value of the effective laser gain
a_{\text{\tiny eff}}\equiv a+\Delta\widetilde{q}
. After convergence, we computed
\Delta\widetilde{q}
and, consequently,
a
. We show in Fig.
13
a representative result of the numerical iteration.

Figure 13:
Overlap function and distribution of internal fields in the spin-glass phase.
Panel (a): overlap function
q(\beta y)
as a function of the rescaled quantity
\beta y
. Due to the presence of a spontaneous magnetization, the value of
q
at the origin
q(0)
is different from
0
. As
\beta y\to\infty
, the overlap function tends to a constant. Panel (b): distribution of internal fields
P(\beta y\to\infty,h)
. The distribution approaches
0
linearly in
|h-J_{0}m|
for
h\approx-J_{0}m
.

Appendix G
Replica calculation of the grand-potential

This Appendix is devoted to the computation of the grand-potential in Eq. (
63
), namely,

\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)=-\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\langle\log Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,,

(195)

where the grandcanonical partition function
Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
is given by

\begin{split}Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}&\equiv\int\text{d}{\bm{x}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\partial_{i}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a))\right)\\
&\times\lvert\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)\rvert\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}})}\\
&=\int_{{\bm{u}},\,{\bm{x}}}\text{e}^{-{\bm{u}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}E^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)+\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}})}\lvert\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)\rvert\,.\end{split}

(196)

In the second line, we used an integral representation of the Dirac delta distribution. The integral
\int_{{\bm{u}},\,{\bm{x}}}\equiv\int_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\frac{\text{d}{\bm{u}}}{(2\pi i)^{N}}\,\int\text{d}{\bm{x}}
is over a
2N
dimensional space of soft spin variables
{\bm{x}}
and auxiliary fields
{\bm{u}}
. The average of the disorder is computed, as in Sec.
V
through the replica trick

\beta\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)=-\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{Nn}\left(\langle(Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}})^{n}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}-1\right)\,.

(197)

The disorder-averaged, replicated grandcanonical partition function
\langle Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}
being

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}})^{n}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}&=\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\Biggl\langle\exp\left[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}x^{\alpha}_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}J_{ij}x_{j}^{\alpha}\right)\right)+\mu\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})\right]\\
&\times\prod_{\alpha}|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\Biggr\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,.\end{split}

(198)

The integral
\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\equiv\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\text{d}{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\text{d}{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}{(2\pi i)^{N}}
is over the space of
n
replicated
2N
dimensional systems of soft spins
{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}
and auxiliary fields
{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}
. The absolute value of the determinant of
\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)
is correlated with the auxiliary fields
{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}
and with the index
\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})
of different replicas. A full treatment of the grand-potential requires taking these correlations into account, for instance by means of conditional averages, as done in
Ros et al. 2019
for spiked tensor models. In our case, however, the analysis is hindered by the single-site term
\partial_{x}E_{I}(x,a)
. To proceed further, we make an approximation by decoupling the gradient of the CIM-SK energy and the determinant of the Hessian among different realizations of the disorder, that is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle Z_{\Omega}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}&\approx\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\left\langle\exp\left[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}x^{\alpha}_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}J_{ij}x_{j}^{\alpha}\right)\right)\right]\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&\times\left\langle\prod_{\alpha}\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,.\end{split}

(199)

Moreover, we neglect the correlations between different replicas when computing the index and the absolute value of the determinant of the Hessian, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\prod_{\alpha}\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&\approx\prod_{\alpha}\left\langle\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\left\langle|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,.\end{split}

(200)

We expect these approximation to allow us to capture the properties of most typical critical points, when no conditioning on their magnetization or energy is taken into account.

G.1
First contribution

We consider the first average over the disorder in Eq. (
199
), which can be computed in a similar way as done in App.
A
. We obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\exp\left[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}x^{\alpha}_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}J_{ij}x_{j}^{\alpha}\right)\right)\right]\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\exp\left[-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+J_{0}\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}x_{j}^{\alpha}\right)+\frac{1}{4N}\sum_{i,j}\left(\sum_{\alpha}u_{i}^{\alpha}x_{j}^{\alpha}+u_{j}^{\beta}x_{i}^{\beta}\right)^{2}\right]\\
&=\exp\left[-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+NJ_{0}\sum_{\alpha}\left(\frac{{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}}}{N}\right)\left(\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}}}{N}\right)+\frac{N}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\left(\frac{{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{u}}^{\beta}}{N}\right)\left(\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta}}{N}\right)+\left(\frac{{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta}}{N}\right)^{2}\right]\right]\,,\end{split}

(201)

where we recall that
\{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{n}
is a set of
N
-dimensional vectors representing the soft spin and auxiliary field configurations of each replica. We then use the Hubbard-Stratonovich transform introduced in Eq. (
125
) to linearize the quadratic term
\left(\frac{{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta}}{N}\right)^{2}
, and we insert the following set of Dirac deltas, together with their integral representations, for each distinct pairs of replica indexes
\alpha,\,\beta
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta(NQ_{\alpha\beta}-{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta})&=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\text{d}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}}{2\pi i}\text{e}^{-N\lambda_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda_{\alpha\beta}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta})}\\
\delta(Nm_{\alpha}-{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}})&=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\text{d}v_{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\,\text{e}^{-Nv_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}+v_{\alpha}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}})}\,.\end{split}

(202)

Equation (
201
) thus becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\text{d}v_{\alpha}\text{d}m_{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\prod_{\alpha\leq\beta}\frac{\text{d}Q_{\alpha\beta}\text{d}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}}{2\pi i}\prod_{\alpha,\beta}\text{d}w_{\alpha\beta}\,\exp\left[-N\left({\bm{m}}\cdot{\bm{v}}+\Tr\left[\bm{\lambda}\text{\bf Q}+\frac{1}{2}{\bm{w}}{\bm{w}}^{\mathrm{T}}\right]\right)\right]\\
&\times\exp\Biggl[-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta})+J_{0}\sum_{\alpha}({\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}})m_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{t}})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}({\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{u}}^{\beta})\\
&-\sum_{\alpha,\beta}w_{\alpha\beta}({\bm{u}}^{\alpha}\cdot{\bm{x}}^{\beta})\Biggr]\,,\end{split}

(203)

where the
n\times n
matrices
\bm{\lambda}
,
Q
,
{\bm{w}}
and the
n
-dimensional vectors
{\bm{m}}
,
{\bm{v}}
have been introduced.

G.2
Second contribution

The terms in the second average in Eq. (
199
) can be computed from the knowledge of
\langle\det\left[z-\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)\right]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}
, with
z
a complex number close to the real axis. In fact, the index
\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}})
can be obtained from the formula
Cavagna et al. 2000

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}})&=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\bigl[\log\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)-i\epsilon]\\
&-\log\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)+i\epsilon]\bigr]\,,\end{split}

(204)

which can be proved using Cauchy’s residues theorem. The branch cut of the complex logarithm function is chosen so that the quantities
\log\lambda_{i}\pm i\epsilon
are on the opposite side of the branch cut for any eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
of
\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)
. For replica
\alpha
, we thus obtain

\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)-i\epsilon]}{\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)+i\epsilon]}\right)^{\mu/2\pi i}\,.

(205)

The absolute value of the determinant can be instead expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|=\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)]\text{e}^{\xi\pi i\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)]\left(\frac{\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)-i\epsilon]}{\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)+i\epsilon]}\right)^{\frac{\xi}{2}}\,,\end{split}

(206)

where
\xi
can be taken to be either equal to
1
or
-1
. This freedom in the choice of the sign will be exploited later on to simplify the calculations, as done in
Yamamura et al. 2024
. Finally, to proceed further, we assume that
\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)]
, and
\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}},a)\pm i\epsilon]
are independent random variables under different realizations of the disorder, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\approx\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{\langle\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)-i\epsilon]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}}{\langle\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)+i\epsilon]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}}\right)^{\frac{\mu}{2\pi i}}\\
&\langle|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\approx\langle\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&\times\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{\langle\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)-i\epsilon]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}}{\langle\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)+i\epsilon]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}}\right)^{\frac{\xi}{2}}\,.\end{split}

(207)

Within this approximation, we thus see that we need to compute the average
\langle\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}
. We compute this next.

G.2.1
Determinant of the Hessian

We express the determinant
\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon]
by means of two sets of independent Grassmann variables
\psi_{i}^{\alpha}
,
\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}

Zinn-Justin 2021
, satisfying the anticommutation relations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\alpha}+\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\alpha}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}&=0\\
\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\psi_{j}^{\alpha}+\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}&=0\\
\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\,\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\alpha}+\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\alpha}\,\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}&=0\,.\end{split}

(208)

We thus write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\det[\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon]\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}=\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\text{d}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\text{d}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left\langle\exp\left[\sum_{i,j}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{J_{ij}}{\sqrt{N}}-\frac{J_{0}}{N}+(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha}_{i},a)\pm i\epsilon)\delta_{ij}\right)\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\right]\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\int_{\bm{\psi}^{\alpha},\,\overline{\bm{\psi}}^{\alpha}}\,\exp\left[\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i\leq j}\left(\overline{\psi}^{\alpha}_{i}\psi^{\alpha}_{j}+\overline{\psi}^{\alpha}_{j}\psi^{\alpha}_{i}\right)^{2}+\sum_{i,j}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{J_{0}}{N}+(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha}_{i},a)\pm i\epsilon)\delta_{ij}\right)\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&=\int_{\bm{\psi}^{\alpha},\,\overline{\bm{\psi}}^{\alpha}}\,\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2N}\left(\sum_{i}\overline{\psi}^{\alpha}_{i}\psi^{\alpha}_{i}\right)^{2}+\sum_{i,j}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{J_{0}}{N}+(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha}_{i},a)\pm i\epsilon)\delta_{ij}\right)\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&=\int_{\bm{\psi}^{\alpha},\,\overline{\bm{\psi}}^{\alpha}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}t^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2\pi/N}}\,\exp\left[-\frac{N}{2}(t^{\alpha})^{2}-it^{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\right)\right]\\
&\times\exp\left[\sum_{i,j}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{J_{0}}{N}+(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha}_{i},a)\pm i\epsilon)\delta_{ij}\right)\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&=\int_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\frac{i\text{d}t^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2\pi/N}}\,\exp\left[\frac{N}{2}(t^{\alpha})^{2}\right]\int_{\bm{\psi}^{\alpha},\,\overline{\bm{\psi}}^{\alpha}}\exp\left[\sum_{i,j}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{J_{0}}{N}+(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon-t^{\alpha})\delta_{ij}\right)\psi_{j}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&=\int_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\frac{i\text{d}t^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2\pi/N}}\,\exp\left[N\left(\frac{1}{2}(t^{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\log\det\left[(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon-t^{\alpha})\delta_{ij}-\frac{J_{0}}{N}\right]\right)\right]\\
&\approx\int_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\text{d}t^{\alpha}\exp\left[N\left(\frac{1}{2}(t^{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\log\det\left[(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon-t^{\alpha})\delta_{ij}\right]\right)\right]\\
&=\int_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\text{d}t^{\alpha}\exp\left[N\left(\frac{1}{2}(t^{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\left(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)\pm i\epsilon-t^{\alpha}\right)\right)\right]\,.\end{split}

(209)

In the second line, we computed the average over the disorder and we used the shorthand notation for the integral
\int_{\bm{\psi}^{\alpha},\,\overline{\bm{\psi}}^{\alpha}}=\int\prod_{i}\text{d}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\text{d}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}
. In the third line, we used the anticommutation properties of the Grassmann variables to compute the square

\sum_{i\leq j}(\overline{\psi_{i}}^{\alpha}\psi_{j}^{\alpha}+\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\alpha}\psi_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}=-\left(\sum_{i}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\alpha}\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\right)^{2}\,.

(210)

In the fourth line, we used a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation. In the fifth line, we changed the integration variable. In the sixth line, we expressed the integral over the Grassmann variable as the determinant of a matrix. In the seventh line, we neglected subleading contribution in
N
. Note that the ferromagnetic coupling is among these subleading contributions. This can be understood from the matrix determinant lemma
Ding and Zhou 2007
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\det\left[\text{\bf A}+\frac{J_{0}}{N}{\bm{t}}\otimes{\bm{t}}\right]&=\left(1+\frac{J_{0}}{N}{\bm{t}}\cdot\text{\bf A}^{-1}{\bm{t}}\right)\det\text{\bf A}\,,\end{split}

(211)

where
{\bm{t}}=[1,\,\ldots,1]^{\mathrm{T}}
is a
N
-dimensional vector and
A
is a real
N\times N
invertible matrix with entries of
O(1)
with respect to
N
. A determinant of this type contributes to the exponential in Eq. (
209
) through

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}&\log\det\left[\text{\bf A}+\frac{J_{0}}{N}{\bm{t}}\otimes{\bm{t}}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\log\left(1+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\Tr[\text{\bf A}^{-1}]\right)+\frac{1}{N}\log\det\text{\bf A}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\log\det\text{\bf A}+O(N^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(212)

we thus see that the ferromagnetic alignment
J_{0}
contributes to the integral in Eq. (
209
) through a term subleading in
N
. From the last line of Eq. (
209
), we anticipate that a saddle point evaluation of the integral is possible. Using this fact, and the approximations in Eq. (
206
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha})}|\det\text{\bf H}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}({\bm{x}}^{\alpha},a)|\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}&\approx\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int\text{d}t^{\alpha}_{0}\,\text{d}t^{\alpha}_{+}\text{d}t^{\alpha}_{-}\exp\Biggl[N\left((t_{0}^{\alpha})^{2}+\left(\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{\mu}{2\pi i}\right)\left((t_{+}^{\alpha})^{2}-(t_{-}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)\right)\\
&+\left(\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{\mu}{2\pi i}\right)\sum_{i}\log\frac{\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)-i\epsilon-t_{+}^{\alpha}}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)+i\epsilon-t_{-}^{\alpha}}+\sum_{i}\log(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x_{i}^{\alpha},a)-t_{0}^{\alpha})\Biggr]\,,\end{split}

(213)

where we neglected subleading contributions in the thermodynamic limit.

G.3
Saddle point evaluation of the grand-potential

We substitute Eq. (
201
) and Eq. (
213
) into the grandcanonical partition function of Eq. (
196
). We can now evaluate the grand-potential
\Omega(\mu)
in the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
through a saddle point integral, as done for the resolvent in App.
A
. We obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)&=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\Omega_{0}-\log\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{d}u^{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\text{e}^{S_{\Omega}[\{x^{\alpha},u^{\alpha}\}]}\right]\\
\Omega_{0}&=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\lambda_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\right]\\
S_{\Omega}&=-\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\lambda_{\alpha\beta}x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}Q_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}-w_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\right]+\sum_{\alpha}[J_{0}m_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+v_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}]\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}\left((t_{0}^{\alpha})^{2}+\left(\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{\mu}{2\pi i}\right)\left((t_{+}^{\alpha})^{2}-(t_{-}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)\right)\\
&+\left(\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{\mu}{2\pi i}\right)\sum_{\alpha}\log\frac{\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-i\epsilon-t_{+}^{\alpha}}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)+i\epsilon-t_{-}^{\alpha}}+\sum_{\alpha}\log(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{0}^{\alpha})\,.\end{split}

(214)

The order parameters satisfy the following set of saddle point equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\alpha\beta}&=\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}&=\frac{1}{2}\langle u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
w_{\alpha\beta}&=-\langle x^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
m_{\alpha}&=\langle x^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
v_{\alpha}&=J_{0}\langle u^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
t_{0}^{\alpha}&=\left\langle\frac{1}{\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{0}^{\alpha}}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\\
t_{\pm}^{\alpha}&=\left\langle\frac{1}{\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha})\mp i\epsilon-t_{\pm}^{\alpha}}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\,,\end{split}

(215)

where the average
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\Omega}
denotes an average over the grandcanonical mean field distribution for the replicated system of spins
x^{\alpha}
and Lagrange multipliers
u^{\alpha}
, defined as,

\langle\ldots\rangle_{\Omega}=\frac{\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{d}u^{\alpha}\,\ldots\text{e}^{S_{\Omega}(\{x^{\alpha},u^{\alpha}\})}}{\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{d}u^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{S_{\Omega}(\{x^{\alpha},u^{\alpha}\})}}\,.

(216)

Note that the saddle point solution to the equation for
t_{0}^{\alpha}
is degenerate, for if
t_{0}^{\alpha}
is a solution, then its complex conjugate is a solution solution too. On the other hand, the presence of the term
\mp i\epsilon
in the equation for
t_{\pm}^{\alpha}
lifts this degeneracy. We thus write
t_{\pm}^{\alpha}\equiv t_{R}^{\alpha}\pm it_{I}^{\alpha}
, separating the real part of
t_{\pm}^{\alpha}
from the imaginary part, while
t_{0}^{\alpha}=t_{R}^{\alpha}\pm it_{I}^{\alpha}
. However, this degeneracy is irrelevant for the computation of the grand-potential
\beta\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)
, because we have freedom on the choice of
\xi=\pm 1
, as mentioned below Eq. (
206
). In fact, it is possible to show that if we choose
t_{0}^{\alpha}=t_{R}\pm it_{I}
and
\xi=\mp 1
, then the expression of
S_{\Omega}
in Eq. (
214
) does not depend on the choice of the stationary solution
t_{0}^{\alpha}
, and it becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\Omega}&=-\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\lambda_{\alpha\beta}x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}Q_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}-w_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\right]+\sum_{\alpha}[J_{0}m_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+v_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}]\\
&+\sum_{\alpha}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left((t_{R}^{\alpha})^{2}-(t_{I}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)+\log\left[(\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{R}^{\alpha})^{2}+(t_{I}^{\alpha})^{2}\right]\right]\\
&+\mu\sum_{\alpha}\left[\frac{t_{R}^{\alpha}t_{I}^{\alpha}}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-i(\epsilon+t_{I}^{\alpha})-t_{R}^{\alpha}}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)+i(\epsilon+t_{I}^{\alpha})-t_{R}^{\alpha}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(217)

The real and imaginary parts
t_{R}^{\alpha}
and
t_{I}^{\alpha}
satisfy the following saddle point equations, which are obtained from Eq. (
215
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}t_{R}^{\alpha}&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left\langle\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{R}^{\alpha}}{(\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{R}^{\alpha})^{2}+(t_{I}^{\alpha}+\epsilon)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\\
t_{I}^{\alpha}&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left\langle\frac{\epsilon+t_{I}^{\alpha}}{(\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{R}^{\alpha})^{2}+(t_{I}^{\alpha}+\epsilon)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\,.\end{split}

(218)

From Eq. (
215
), we see that
t_{R}^{\alpha}\pm it_{I}^{\alpha}
satisfy the same self consistent equations for the resolvent, evaluated at the origin, of a random matrix drawn from the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble, whose diagonal elements are perturbed by
\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)
, with the
x^{\alpha}
following the distribution defined in the average
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\Omega}
in Eq. (
216
). We now assume that
t_{I}^{\alpha}=0
which, as explained in
Yamamura et al. 2024
, implies that the spectral density of the Hessian of the most abundant critical points of the system is zero at the origin. We checked through the numerical solution of the saddle point equation that this solution is robust when a nonzero value for the imaginary part is allowed. Within this approximation, the contribution proportional to
\mu
to the action
S_{\Omega}
in Eq. (
217
) becomes, in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mu\sum_{\alpha}\left[\frac{t_{R}^{\alpha}t_{I}^{\alpha}}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-i(\epsilon+t_{I}^{\alpha})-t_{R}^{\alpha}}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)+i(\epsilon+t_{I}^{\alpha})-t_{R}^{\alpha}}\right]\ &=\mu\sum_{\alpha}\Theta(-(\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a)-t_{R}^{\alpha}))\\
&\equiv\mu\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x^{\alpha})\,,\end{split}

(219)

where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside step function, and we have chosen the branch cut of the logarithm to lie on the positive side of the real axis. This choice has a physical justification: in this way, in the limit
\mu\to-\infty
the energy
\beta E_{\Omega}
selects for configurations
x^{\alpha}
located at the minima of the energy function
\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha})
. The last equality defines the mean field index function
\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)
.

We now present the final result for the replica calculation of the grandcanonical partition function. We first find convenient to make a change of variable, replacing the matrices
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}
and
w_{\alpha\beta}
with a new pair of matrices
A_{\alpha\beta}
and
C_{\alpha\beta}
, defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{\alpha\beta}&\equiv-t_{R}^{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\beta}-w_{\alpha\beta}\\
C_{\alpha\beta}&\equiv 2\lambda_{\alpha\beta}\,.\end{split}

(220)

With this substitution and our ansatz
t_{I}^{\alpha}=0
, the expression of the grand-potential in Eq. (
214
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)&=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\Omega_{0}-\log\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{d}u^{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\text{e}^{S_{\Omega}[\{x^{\alpha},u^{\alpha}\}]}\right]\\
\Omega_{0}&=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[C_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}+A_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\right]+\sum_{\alpha}A_{\alpha\alpha}t_{R}^{\alpha}\\
S_{\Omega}&=-\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha})+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}C_{\alpha\beta}x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}Q_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}+A_{\alpha\beta}u^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\right]+\sum_{\alpha}[J_{0}m_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+v_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}]\\
&+\sum_{\alpha}\log|\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},\,a+t_{R}^{\alpha})|+\mu\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x^{\alpha})\,.\end{split}

(221)

The order parameters satisfy the following set of saddle point equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\alpha\beta}&=\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
C_{\alpha\beta}&=\langle u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
A_{\alpha\beta}&=\langle x^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}-t_{R}^{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
m_{\alpha}&=\langle x^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
v_{\alpha}&=J_{0}\langle u^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
t_{R}^{\alpha}&=\left\langle\frac{1}{\lvert\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha})\rvert}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\,.\end{split}

(222)

These equations are evaluated in the replica-symmetric phase in the next Section.

G.4
Replica symmetric ansatz

We make the following ansatz for the structure of the order parameters in Eq. (
222
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{\alpha\beta}&=A_{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}+A_{o}(1-\delta_{\alpha\beta})=\Delta A\delta_{\alpha\beta}+A_{o}\\
C_{\alpha\beta}&=\Delta C\delta_{\alpha\beta}+C_{o}\\
Q_{\alpha\beta}&=\Delta q\delta_{\alpha\beta}+q_{o}\\
m_{\alpha}&=m\\
v_{\alpha}&=v\\
t_{R}^{\alpha}&=t_{R}\,,\end{split}

(223)

and we compute the exponential weight

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int&\prod_{\alpha}\frac{\text{d}x^{\alpha}\text{d}u^{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\text{e}^{S_{\Omega}[\{x^{\alpha},\,u^{\alpha}\}]}=\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\left(\prod_{\alpha}|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R})|\right)\\
&\times\exp\Biggl[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}\left[\Delta C(x^{\alpha})^{2}+2\Delta Ax^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+\Delta q(u^{\alpha})^{2}\right]+\sum_{\alpha}[x^{\alpha}v+J_{0}mu^{\alpha}]\\
&-\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\partial_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R})+\mu\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x^{\alpha})+\frac{1}{2}\left[C_{o}\left(\sum_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\right)^{2}+2A_{o}\left(\sum_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\right)\left(\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\right)+q_{o}\left(\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\right)^{2}\right]\Biggr]\\
&=\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\int\frac{\text{d}h_{1}\text{d}h_{2}}{2\pi}\,\text{e}^{-\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{h_{2}^{2}}{2}}\prod_{\alpha}|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R})|\exp\Biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta C(x^{\alpha})^{2}+2\Delta Ax^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+\Delta q(u^{\alpha})^{2}\right]+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x^{\alpha})\\
&+\left(v+h_{1}\frac{A_{o}}{\sqrt{q_{o}}}+h_{2}\sqrt{C_{o}-\frac{A_{o}^{2}}{q_{o}}}\right)x^{\alpha}-\left(\partial_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R})-J_{0}m-h_{1}\sqrt{q_{o}}\right)u^{\alpha}\Biggr]\\
&\equiv\int_{{\bm{x}}^{\alpha},\,{\bm{u}}^{\alpha}}\int\frac{\text{d}h_{1}\text{d}h_{2}}{2\pi}\,\text{e}^{-\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{h_{2}^{2}}{2}}\prod_{\alpha}|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{\alpha},h_{1})|\exp\Biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta C(x^{\alpha})^{2}+2\Delta Ax^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}+\Delta q(u^{\alpha})^{2}\right]+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x^{\alpha})\\
&+x^{\alpha}F(h_{1},h_{2})-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x^{\alpha},h_{1})u^{\alpha}\Biggr]\\
&=\overline{\overline{\Biggl(\int\frac{\text{d}x\text{d}u}{2\pi}\,|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})|\exp\Biggl[-\frac{\Delta q}{2}\left(u-i\frac{\Delta Ax-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})}{\Delta q}\right)^{2}}}\\
&\overline{\overline{+\frac{1}{2}\Delta Cx^{2}+xF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)-\frac{(\Delta Ax-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}))^{2}}{2\Delta q}\Biggr]\Biggr)^{n}}}\\
&=\overline{\overline{\left(\int\text{d}x\,,\frac{|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})|}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta q}}\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\Delta Cx^{2}+xF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)-\frac{(\Delta Ax-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}))^{2}}{2\Delta q}\right]\right)^{n}}}\,,\end{split}

(224)

where in the second equality, we have used a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation. In the third equality, we have defined an effective energy
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
and a field
F(h_{1},h_{2})
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)&\equiv E_{I}(x,a+t_{R})-J_{0}mx-\sqrt{q_{o}}hx\\
F(h_{1},h_{2})&\equiv v+h_{1}\frac{A_{o}}{\sqrt{q_{o}}}+h_{2}\sqrt{C_{o}-\frac{A_{o}^{2}}{q_{o}}}\,.\end{split}

(225)

Finally in the last equality, we have rotated the integration domain of the fields
u^{\alpha}
in the complex plane, defined the Gaussian average
\overline{\overline{\cdots}}\equiv\int\frac{\text{d}h_{1}\text{d}h_{2}}{2\pi}\text{e}^{-\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{h_{2}^{2}}{2}}
and observed that the integrals over the different replicated fields factorize. Note that within the replica symmetric ansatz, the distribution of the auxiliary field
u
is Gaussian, and it can be integrated out. The last line defines, up to a normalization constant, the single spin probability distribution
P^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}_{\Omega}(x,h_{1},h_{2})
as

P_{\Omega}(x,h_{1},h_{2})\propto|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})|\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\Delta Cx^{2}+xF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)-\frac{(\Delta Ax-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}))^{2}}{2\Delta q}\right]\,.

(226)

Within the replica-symmetric ansatz, the saddle point equations in Eq. (
222
) become

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta A&=\overline{\overline{\langle xu\rangle_{\Omega}-\langle x\rangle_{\Omega}\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}}}-t_{R}\\
A_{o}&=\overline{\overline{\langle x\rangle_{\Omega}\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}}}\\
\Delta C&=\overline{\overline{\langle u^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}-\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}}\\
C_{o}&=\overline{\overline{\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}}\\
\Delta q&=\overline{\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}-\langle x\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}}\\
q_{o}&=\overline{\overline{\langle x\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}}\\
m&=\overline{\overline{\langle x\rangle_{\Omega}}}\\
v&=-J_{0}\overline{\overline{\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}}}\,,\end{split}

(227)

computing the Gaussian average over the field
u
yields the set of equations in Eq. (
70
) of the main text.

G.4.1
Alternative expression of
P_{\Omega}

An alternative expression of the probability distribution
P_{\Omega}(x,h_{1},h_{2})
can be obtained by evaluating the grand-potential average of a generic test function
f(x)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle f\rangle_{\Omega}&=\int\text{d}x\,P(x,h_{1},h_{2})f(x)\\
&\propto\int\text{d}x\,|\partial^{2}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})|\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta C-\frac{\Delta A^{2}}{\Delta q}\right)x^{2}+xF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)\right]\\
&\times\exp\left[-\frac{\left(\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\right)^{2}}{2\Delta q}+\frac{\Delta Ax}{\Delta q}\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\right]f(x)\\
&=\int\text{d}x\,\int\text{d}h_{0}\,\delta(h_{0}-\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}})|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}|\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta C-\frac{\Delta A^{2}}{\Delta q}\right)x^{2}+xF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)\right]\\
&\times\exp\left[-\frac{h_{0}^{2}}{2\Delta q}+\frac{\Delta Ax}{\Delta q}h_{0}\right]f(x)\\
&=\int\text{d}h_{0}\,\text{e}^{-\frac{h_{0}^{2}}{2\Delta q}}\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})]}\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta C-\frac{\Delta A^{2}}{\Delta q}\right)y^{2}+yF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)+\frac{\Delta Ay}{\Delta q}h_{0}\right]f(y)\\
&\propto\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})}\text{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta C-\frac{\Delta A^{2}}{\Delta q}\right)y^{2}+yF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)+\frac{\Delta Ay}{\Delta q}h_{0}}f(y)\right\rangle_{h_{0}}\,,\end{split}

(228)

where we made use of the properties of the Dirac delta, and we have defined in the last line a Gaussian average over the field
h_{0}
, with variance
\Delta q
. We can thus give an alternative expression of the single-spin grand-potential probability density as

P_{\Omega}(x,h_{1},h_{2})\propto\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}\text{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta C-\frac{\Delta A^{2}}{\Delta q}\right)y^{2}+yF(h_{1},h_{2})+\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)+\frac{\Delta Ay}{\Delta q}h_{0}}\delta(x-y)\right\rangle_{h_{0}}\,

(229)

where
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
is defined in (
225
).

G.4.2
The effective energy is convex if and only if supersymmetry is preserved

If the effective grand-potential energy
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1})
is a convex function of
x
for all the realizations of the noise
h_{1}
, i.e. if
a+t_{R}\leq 0
, then supersymmetry is preserved, namely
\Delta A=A_{o}=C_{o}=\Delta C=v=0
. This can be seen through a self-consistent condition, by evaluating the saddle point equations around the supersymmetric point. For the parameter
v
, we get in fact,

\displaystyle\begin{split}v&=\frac{J_{0}}{\Delta q}\overline{\overline{\langle\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\rangle_{\Omega}}}\\
&=\frac{J_{0}}{\Delta q}\overline{\overline{\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}h_{0}\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)}\right\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=\frac{J_{0}}{\Delta q}\overline{\overline{\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle_{h_{0}}}}=0\,.\end{split}

(230)

In the second equality, we used the alternative definition of the single-spin distribution
P_{\Omega}
in the supersymmetric phase, given by Eq. (
229
). In the third equality, we used the fact that, since
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
is a convex function of
x
for all
h
, there is one critical point for every realization of the Gaussian field
h_{0}
, and the mean field index evaluates to zero because of the convexity of the effective energy. In the last equality, we used the fact that the mean of
h_{0}
is, by definition,
0
. This calculation implies that the self-consistent conditions
C_{o}=A_{o}=0
are verified too, since these order parameters are all proportional to
\langle\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\rangle_{\Omega}
. The self-consistent condition for the parameter
\Delta C
reads instead

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Delta C=-\frac{1}{\Delta q}\left[1-\Delta q^{-1}\overline{\overline{\langle\left(\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\right)^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}}}\right]\\
&=-\frac{1}{\Delta q}\\
&+\Delta q^{-2}\overline{\overline{\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}h_{0}^{2}\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)}\right\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=-\frac{1}{\Delta q}+\frac{1}{\Delta q^{2}}\overline{\overline{\langle h_{0}^{2}\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=0\,,\end{split}

(231)

where we used a similar reasoning to the one adopted in Eq. (
230
). Finally, we verify the self-consistent condition for
\Delta A
in the following way

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta A&=\overline{\overline{\Delta q^{-1}\langle x\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}\rangle_{\Omega}}}-t_{R}=\overline{\overline{\Biggl\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}\Biggl[\frac{h_{0}y}{\Delta q}-\frac{1}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(y,h_{1}+h_{0})}\Biggr]\Biggr\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=\overline{\overline{\Biggl\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}\Biggl[\frac{\text{d}y}{\text{d}h_{0}}-\frac{1}{\partial_{x}^{2}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(y,h_{1}+h_{0})}\Biggr]\Biggr\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=-\overline{\overline{\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}h_{0}}\left(\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\rvert_{x=y(h_{0})}\right)\right\rangle_{h_{0}}}}=0\,,\end{split}

(232)

where the third equality follows from integration by parts, while in the final equality we used the fact that
y
is a critical point of the function
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}
. These calculations demonstrate that when
a+t_{R}\leq 0
, the supersymmetry condition is self-consistently verified. Note that this is true regardless of the magnetization
m
being zero or not. Moreover, these self-consistent conditions hold also in the case
\Delta q\to 0
, when
h_{0}
concentrates around the origin.

On the other hand, the self consistent condition for a supersymmetric phase is violated when the mean field potential
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
is non-convex for some value of
h
, i.e. when
a+t_{R}>0
. Let us compute, in this case, the quantity
\Delta C+C_{o}
. There is a range of values of
h_{0}
for which the number of critical points of
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})
is three: two minima and a saddle. The equality obtained in the supersymmetric case becomes therefore an upper bound, where only one minimum among the three critical points is counted. We thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta C+C_{o}&=-\frac{1}{\Delta q}\left[1-\frac{1}{\Delta q}\overline{\overline{\langle(\partial_{x}E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}})^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}}}\right]\\
&>-\frac{1}{\Delta q}+\frac{1}{\Delta q}\overline{\overline{\langle h^{2}_{0}\rangle_{h_{0}}}}=0\,,\end{split}

(233)

which demonstrates the violation of the supersymmetric self-consistent condition.

G.5
\Omega(\mu)=0
in the supersymmetric phase

In the supersymmetric phase,
\Delta A=A_{o}=\Delta C=C_{o}=v=0
. Through a calculation similar to the one performed in Sec.
G.4.1
we can show, starting from Eq. (
221
), that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega(\mu)&=-\overline{\overline{\log Z_{\Omega,\text{\tiny{RS}}}({\bm{h}})}}\\
&=-\overline{\overline{\log\left\langle\sum_{y\in\Crt_{x}\left[E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h_{1}+h_{0})\right]}\text{e}^{\mu\mathcal{I}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(y)}\right\rangle_{h_{0}}}}\\
&=-\overline{\overline{\log 1}}\\
&=0\,.\end{split}

(234)

In passing from the second to the third equality in the equation above, we exploited the fact that in the supersymmetric phase the effective energy
E_{\scriptstyle\Omega,\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
is a convex function of
x
for all the realizations of
h
, and thus it thus admits a unique critical point, as a function of
x
, of mean field index
0
.

G.6
Derivation of Eq. (
77
)

In this Appendix we discuss how some order parameters appearing in the Kac-Rice calculation can be interpreted, in the replica symmetric phase, as susceptibilities of the grand-potential
\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)
with respect to perturbations of the single-site energy
E_{I}
described by Eq. (
76
). The different perturbations independently change the mean-field grand-potential action
S_{\Omega}
in Eq. (
221
). The perturbation proportional to
\epsilon_{A}
changes
S_{\Omega}
by an amount
\Delta S_{\Omega,\,A}
, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Delta S_{\Omega,\,A}\equiv\epsilon_{A}\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\\
&-\sum_{\alpha}\log\frac{|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha}+\epsilon_{A})|}{|\partial^{2}_{x}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha})|}\\
&+\mu\sum_{\alpha}\Theta(-\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha}+\epsilon_{A}))\\
&-\mu\sum_{\alpha}\Theta(-\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha}))\,,\end{split}

(235)

therefore, the derivative with respect to
\epsilon_{A}
of the grand-potential becomes, within the replica symmetric ansatz,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\epsilon_{A}}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)\rvert_{\epsilon_{A}=0}&=-\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha}\Biggl[\langle u^{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\frac{1}{\lvert\partial_{x}^{2}E_{I}(x^{\alpha},a+t_{R}^{\alpha})\rvert}\right\rangle_{\Omega}\Biggr]\\
&=-\overline{\overline{\langle ux\rangle_{\Omega}}}+\frac{1}{2}t_{R}\\
&=-A_{o}-\Delta A\,,\end{split}

(236)

thus proving the first relation in Eq. (
77
). The perturbation proportional to
\epsilon_{C}
introduces a random fields on each soft spin site. It thus couples different replicas. The associated change in the mean field energy
\Delta S_{\Omega,\,C}
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta S_{\Omega,\,C}=\epsilon_{C}\sum_{\alpha,\,\beta}u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\,.\end{split}

(237)

The derivative with respect to
\epsilon_{C}
of the grand-potential becomes

\begin{split}\partial_{\epsilon_{C}}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)\rvert_{\epsilon_{C}=0}&=-\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha,\,\beta}\langle u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
&=-\left[\overline{\overline{\langle u^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}}}-\overline{\overline{\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}}\right]\\
&=-\Delta C\,.\end{split}

(238)

This is the second relation in Eq. (
77
). Finally, the perturbation proportional to
\epsilon_{v}
yields a change in the mean field grand-potential energy
\Delta S_{\Omega,\,v}
given by

\Delta S_{\Omega,\,v}=J_{0}\epsilon_{v}\sum_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}\,,

(239)

and the associated derivative of the grand-potential reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\epsilon_{v}}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)\rvert_{\epsilon_{v}=0}&=-\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}J_{0}\sum_{\alpha}\langle u^{\alpha}\rangle_{\Omega}\\
&=-J_{0}\overline{\overline{\langle u\rangle_{\Omega}}}\\
&=-v\,,\end{split}

(240)

which is the last relation in Eq. (
77
) in the main text.

G.7
Direct sampling of critical points in finite size systems

The direct sampling of critical points in finite size system is performed following the algorithm of
Yamamura et al. 2024
. Given a system of
N
soft spins, we sample
N_{J}
realizations of the connectivity matrix
\text{\bf J}^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}
. For each realization of the connectivity matrix, we initialize the system from random initial conditions, and we find the closest critical point using Newton-conjugate gradient descent on the squared norm of the gradient of the energy,
\left[\bm{\nabla}E({\bm{x}},a)\right]^{2}
, with
E({\bm{x}},a)
given by Eq. (
2
). When sampling minima, we use Newton-conjugate gradient descent on the energy
E({\bm{x}},a)
of the system. This procedure is iterated for
N_{\text{\tiny{samples}}}
times for
each
realization. A deduplication procedure is employed to avoid counting the same critical point multiple times. Pairs of critical points whose Euclidean distance is smaller then
10^{-6}
are identified as the same critical point. To produce the scatter-plots in Fig.
5
, we used
N=12
,
N_{J}=10
and
N_{\text{\tiny{samples}}}=5\times 10^{5}
.

G.8
Argument for the stability of the paramagnetic solutions of
\Omega(\mu)

The following argument rationalizes the finding that the most abundant critical point are paramagnetic in the supersymmetry-broken region, even for moderately large values of the ferromagnetic alignment
J_{0}
. Let us consider small perturbations to the magnetization
\delta m^{\alpha}
and to the associated Lagrange multiplier
i\delta v^{\alpha}
around the paramagnetic, replica-symmetric, supersymmetry-breaking saddle point of the grand-potential
\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)
in Eq. (
221
), where
m_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}=0
. The perturbation components
\delta m_{\alpha}
are real, while the components
i\delta v_{\alpha}
are pure imaginary numbers, since the steepest descent path for the auxiliary variables
{\bm{v}}
across which the saddle point is evaluated is orthogonal to the real axis. The change of the grand-potential up to quadratic order in these perturbations is

n\delta\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}(\mu)=\begin{bmatrix}\delta{\bm{m}}\\
\delta{\bm{v}}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}m_{\beta}}&i\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}\partial v_{\beta}}\\
i\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial v_{\alpha}\partial m_{\beta}}&-\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial v_{\alpha}\partial v_{\beta}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\delta{\bm{m}}\\
\delta{\bm{v}}\end{bmatrix}\,,

(241)

where we have defined a
2n\times 2n
matrix, made up by four
n\times n
blocks. Their entries read

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}\partial m_{\beta}}&=-J_{0}^{2}\overline{\overline{\langle u^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}=-J_{0}^{2}\Delta C\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial m_{\alpha}\partial v_{\beta}}&=\left(1-J_{0}\overline{\overline{\langle ux\rangle_{\Omega}}}\right)\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
