&=\left(1-J_{0}\Delta A+J_{0}t_{R}\right)\delta_{\alpha\beta}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega^{\text{\tiny{SK}}}}{\partial v_{\alpha}\partial v_{\beta}}&=-\overline{\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\Omega}}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}=-\Delta q\delta_{\alpha\beta}\,,\end{split}

(242)

where we used the fact that in the replica-symmetric, paramagnetic phase
v=m=q_{o}=C_{o}=A_{o}=0
. All
n\times n
sub-blocks are diagonal. The paramagnetic solution is stable if all the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix in Eq. (
241
) are positive. To check whether this condition is obeyed, let us observe that when integrating numerically the saddle point equations in Eq. (
70
) for
J_{0}=0
, we obtain that
\Delta C<0
both for
\mu=0
and for a large and negative value of
\mu
, as shown in Fig.
14
.

Figure 14:
Negative values of
\Delta C
in the paramagnetic, supersymmetry-breaking phase.
Plot of
\Delta C
as a function of the laser gain in the absence of ferromagnetic bias, obtained from the numerical solution of Eq. (
70
). We use two distinct values of the chemical potential
\mu
, corresponding to the properties of the most abundant critical points and the properties of the most abundant minima. The latter are obtained by taking a large negative value of
\mu
.
\Delta C
is less than zero in the supersymmetry-broken phase.

We thus assume that in the paramagnetic phase,
\Delta C<0
. Then, the top left block of the Hessian in Eq. (
241
), associated to fluctuations of the magnetization is positive definite. The matrix in Eq. (
241
) has therefore one eigenvalue with a positive definite real part, and thus the paramagnetic solution is stable as long as both eigenvalues are positive. The stability condition becomes thus

\det\begin{bmatrix}-J_{0}^{2}\Delta C&i(1-J_{0}\Delta A+J_{0}t_{R})\\
i(1-J_{0}\Delta A+J_{0}t_{R})&\Delta q\end{bmatrix}>0\,,

(243)

which yields

\left[1-J_{0}(\Delta A+t_{R})\right]^{2}-J_{0}^{2}\Delta C\Delta q>0\,.

(244)

This condition is satisfied for
\Delta C<0
. This stability analysis rationalizes our numerical observation that the most abundant critical points are paramagnetic even in presence of a net ferromagnetic coupling strength.

Appendix H
Dynamical mean field theory

In this Appendix we derive the dynamical mean field equations for the coherent Ising machine, presented in Eq. (
79
), using the dynamical cavity method
Agoritsas et al. 2018
. The starting point is the gradient descent dynamics for a system of
N
spins given by Eq. (
4
), which we rewrite here as

\tau\dot{x}_{i}=-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{i},a(t))+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{j}x_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j}J_{ij}x_{j}\,.

(245)

We want to describe the effective dynamics of a single spin of the system. To do so, we consider a system made by
N+1
spins, where an additional degree of freedom
x_{0}(t)
has been added. We refer to this additional spin as the cavity variable. The new spin interacts with the other spins in the system through the ferromagnetic coupling
J_{0}/N
and through a set of symmetric, disordered couplings
J_{0j}=J_{j0}
, which are independent, identically distributed Gaussian variables of mean
0
and unit variance. The cavity variable perturbs the dynamics of the other spins in the system through a field
h_{i}(t)
, which reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}h_{i}(t)&\equiv\frac{J_{0}}{N}x_{0}(t)+\frac{J_{i0}}{\sqrt{N}}x_{0}(t)\\
\ &=\frac{J_{i0}}{\sqrt{N}}x_{0}(t)+O(N^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(246)

in the second line, we have observed that the field
h_{i}(t)
is of order
O(N^{-1/2})
. Let us denote by
\tilde{x}_{i}(t)
the trajectories of spins
i=1,\ldots,N
perturbed by the cavity degree of freedom. The equation of motion of the perturbed spins is

\begin{split}\tau\dot{\widetilde{x}}_{i}&=-\partial_{x}E_{I}(\tilde{x}_{i},a(t))+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{j\neq 0}\tilde{x}_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j\neq 0}J_{ij}\tilde{x}_{j}\\
&+h_{i}(t)\,,\end{split}

(247)

with
h_{i}(t)
given by Eq. (
246
). On the other hand, the equation of motion of the cavity variable is thus

\tau\dot{x}_{0}=-E_{I}^{\prime}(x_{0},a(t))+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i\neq 0}\tilde{x}_{i}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i\neq 0}J_{0i}\tilde{x}_{i}\,.

(248)

All the terms in Eq. (
245
), (
247
) and Eq. (
248
) are of order
O(1)
. On the other hand we see from Eq. (
246
) that
h_{i}(t)\sim O(N^{-1/2})
. We can thus treat the interaction between spin
i
and spin
0
as a small perturbation to the trajectory of spin
i
in the system without the cavity variable,
x_{i}(t)
. Using linear response theory, we thus write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{x}_{i}&(t)\approx x_{i}(t)+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\,\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{j}(\tau)}\Bigg\rvert_{h_{j}=0}h_{j}(\tau)\\
&=x_{i}(t)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\,\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{j}(\tau)}\Bigg\rvert_{h_{j}=0}J_{j0}x_{0}(\tau)\,,\end{split}

(249)

Where
\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{j}(t^{\prime})}\Big\lvert_{h_{j}=0}
is the variation of the value
x_{i}(t)
under a small change in the field
h_{j}(t^{\prime})
acting on spin
j
, evaluated in the absence of any external field. Substituting Eq. (
249
) into Eq. (
248
) we obtain, to leading order in
N
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau\dot{x}_{0}&\approx-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x_{0},a(t))+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i\neq 0}x_{i}(t)\\
&+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i\neq 0}J_{0i}x_{i}\\
&+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j\neq 0}\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{j}(\tau)}J_{0j}J_{i0}x_{0}(\tau)\,.\end{split}

(250)

This equation describes the dynamics of the cavity variable in terms of the dynamics of the unperturbed spins
x_{i}(t)
, which are independent from the dynamics of the cavity. The spins
x_{i}(t)
can be considered as a set random variables under different realizations of the disordered couplings
J_{ij}
. In the thermodynamic limit, the cross-correlations among two spin
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
read

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\left(x_{i}(t)-\left\langle x_{i}(t)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\right)\left(x_{j}(t^{\prime})-\left\langle x_{j}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\right)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k,l}\left\langle J_{ik}J_{jl}x_{i}(t)x_{j}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\delta_{ij}\left\langle x_{i}(t)x_{j}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}+O(N^{-1})\,.\end{split}

(251)

This equation demonstrates that in the thermodynamic limit the dynamics of two different spins under different realizations of the quenched disorder are independent of each other. The term
N^{-1}J_{0}\sum_{i\neq 0}x_{i}(t)
in Eq. (
250
) can then be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i}x_{i}(t)&=\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i}\left\langle x_{i}(t)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&+\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i}x_{i}(t)-\left\langle x_{i}(t)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\frac{J_{0}}{N}\sum_{i}\left\langle x_{i}(t)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}+O(N^{-1/2})\\
&\equiv J_{0}m(t)+O(N^{-1/2})\,.\end{split}

(252)

In the second equality, we applied the central limit theorem, while the third equality defines the time-dependent magnetization of the system
m(t)
. Similarly, the response term in Eq. (
250
) concentrates around its average value in the thermodynamic limit:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\,\sum_{i,j}\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{j}(\tau)}\Biggr\rvert_{h_{j}=0}J_{0i}J_{j0}x_{0}(\tau)\\
&\approx\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\,\left\langle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\frac{\delta x_{i}(t)}{\delta h_{i}(\tau)}\Biggr\rvert_{h_{j}=0}\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}x_{0}(\tau)\\
&\equiv\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau\,R(t,\tau)x_{0}(\tau)\,,\end{split}

(253)

where the second line defines the response function
R(t,\tau)
. Finally, the second term in Eq. (
250
) can by written, using the central limit theorem, as a Gaussian noise
\xi(t)
with mean
\langle\xi(t)\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}=0
and variance

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\xi(t)\xi(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j}\left\langle J_{0i}J_{0j}x_{i}(t)x_{j}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\left\langle x_{i}(t)x_{i}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\equiv C(t,t^{\prime})\,,\end{split}

(254)

where the last equality defines the correlation function
C(t,t^{\prime})
. Finally, we observe that, since the system is fully connected, the dynamics of one spin is equivalent to the dynamics of any other spin. This is true for the dynamics of the cavity variable as well. The index
0
in Eq. (
250
) can then be dropped, as well as the index
i
in the second-to-last lines of Eqs. (
252
), (
253
), and (
254
). We thus obtain an effective equation of motion for a spin
x(t)
in the coherent Ising machine, namely

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau\dot{x}(t)&=-\partial_{x}E_{I}(x,a)\\
&+J_{0}m(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}\tau R(t,\tau)x(\tau)+\xi(\tau)\\
\left\langle\xi(t)\xi(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}&=C(t,t^{\prime})=\left\langle x(t)x(t^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
m(t)&=\left\langle x(t)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\\
R(t,t^{\prime})&=\left\langle\frac{\delta x(t)}{\delta h(t^{\prime})}\Biggr\rvert_{h=0}\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}=\left\langle\frac{\delta x(t)}{\delta\xi(t^{\prime})}\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,.\end{split}

(255)

In the last line, we used the fact that the variation of
x(t)
with respect to a variation in an external field
h(t^{\prime})
can equivalently be obtained by making an infinitesimal change in the noise at time
t^{\prime}
. Equation (
255
) is the dynamical mean field theory of the coherent Ising machine. It provides a set of self-consistent equations that can be solved to obtain the temporal correlation
C(t,t^{\prime})
, magnetization
m(t)
, and response
R(t,t^{\prime})
given initial conditions on
x
. We show how to find such solutions next.

H.1
Numerical solution of the dynamical mean field theory

Equation (
255
) can be solved numerically for any initial condition
x(0)
on a discrete temporal grid,
t_{i}=i\Delta t
with
i=0\,,\ldots,N_{\text{steps}}
, by means of an iterative self-consistent scheme
Eissfeller and Opper 1992
;
Eissfeller and Opper 1994
;
Roy et al. 2019
. Starting from an initial guess of the function
m(t_{i})
,
C(t_{i},t_{j})
and
R(t_{i},t_{j})
we can generate a set of
N_{\text{traj}}
independent trajectories
\{x_{\alpha}(t_{i})\}_{\alpha=1}^{N_{\text{traj}}}
using an Euler scheme. To do this, we first generate and store
N_{\text{traj}}\times N_{\text{steps}}
realizations of the noise, with correlations
\langle\xi_{\alpha}(t_{i})\xi_{\beta}(t_{j})\rangle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}C(t_{i},t_{j})
. We can then use the self-consistent equations for
m(t)
and
C(t,t^{\prime})
given by Eq. (
255
) to compute the new values of the correlation function
C_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})
and of the magnetization
m_{\text{new}}(t_{i})
, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})&=\frac{1}{N_{\text{traj}}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{\text{traj}}}x_{\alpha}(t_{i})x_{\alpha}(t_{j})\\
m_{\text{new}}(t_{i})&=\frac{1}{N_{\text{traj}}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{\text{traj}}}x_{\alpha}(t_{i})\,.\end{split}

(256)

To compute the response function of the system, we can use the Novikov relation
Novikov 1965
;
Roy et al. 2019
, which relates the response function to the realizations of the noise along a trajectory as

R(t,t^{\prime})=\left\langle x(t)\int\text{d}\tau\,C^{-1}(t^{\prime},\tau)\xi(\tau)\right\rangle_{\text{\bf J}}\,,

(257)

where
C^{-1}(t,t^{\prime})
is the inverse of the correlation function
C(t,t^{\prime})
, defined by the identity
\int\text{d}\tau C(t,\tau)C^{-1}(\tau,t^{\prime})=\delta(t-t^{\prime})
. For a discrete time grid and a finite number of trajectories, Novikov relation reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})&=\frac{1}{N_{\text{traj}}\Delta t}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{\text{traj}}}x_{\alpha}(t_{i})\\
&\times\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{steps}}}C^{-1}(t_{i},t_{k})\xi_{\alpha}(t_{k})\,.\end{split}

(258)

Once the new values of the order parameters
C_{\text{new}}
,
R_{\text{new}}
and
m_{\text{new}}
are known, we update the current values of the order parameter using a linear interpolation between the current values and the new values with parameter
\gamma
, namely

\displaystyle\begin{split}C(t_{i},t_{j})&\leftarrow\gamma C_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})+(1-\gamma)C(t_{i},t_{j})\\
R(t_{i},t_{j})&\leftarrow\gamma R_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})+(1-\gamma)R(t_{i},t_{j})\\
m(t_{i})&\leftarrow\gamma m_{\text{new}}(t_{i})+(1-\gamma)m(t_{i})\,.\end{split}

(259)

This interpolation scheme is used to avoid large jumps as well as oscillations between updates. Once the order parameters are updated, the procedure is repeated until convergence is achieved. As a convergence criterion, we impose

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{N_{\text{steps}}}\sum_{i}(m(t_{i})-m_{\text{new}}(t_{i}))^{2}\\
&+\frac{1}{N_{\text{steps}}^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\left(C(t_{i},t_{j})-C_{\text{new}}(t_{i},t_{j})\right)^{2}\leq\epsilon\,,\end{split}

(260)

with
\epsilon>0
. The complexity of this algorithm is of order
O(N_{\text{traj}}\times N_{\text{steps}}^{2})
. The results presented in the main text are obtained using
N_{\text{traj}}=20\,000
,
T/\Delta t=5\,000
,
\Delta t=0.15
,
\gamma=0.3
,
\epsilon=10^{-4}
.

Appendix I
Wishart planted ensemble

In this Section, we compute the average of the single spin distribution at the global minima of the CIM-WPE in Eq. (
98
). We begin by rewriting the single spin distribution as the derivative of a modified free energy function,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\delta(x_{i}-x)\rangle_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}&=\frac{1}{N}\Biggl\langle\frac{\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\text{d}y_{i}\,\sum_{i}\delta(y_{i}-x)\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\text{\bf y})}}{\int\prod_{i}\text{d}y_{i}\,\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\text{\bf y})}}\Biggr\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\Biggl\langle\partial_{\ell}\log\int\text{d}\mathbf{y}\,\text{e}^{-\beta E^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}+\ell\sum_{i}\delta(y_{i}-x)}\Biggr\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\Biggl|_{\ell=0}\\
&\equiv\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\ell}\langle\log Z_{\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}|_{\ell=0}\,,\end{split}

(261)

where the last line defines a modified partition function
Z_{\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
. To compute the average over the disorder, we employ the replica trick described in Eq. (
21
), which yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\ell}\langle\log Z_{\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}|_{\ell=0}&=\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\ell}\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\langle(Z_{\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}})^{n}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\rvert_{\ell=0}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\ell}\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\Biggl\langle\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\text{d}\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha}E^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\mathbf{y}^{\alpha})+\ell\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)}\Biggr\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\Biggl|_{\ell=0}\,.\end{split}

(262)

Upon substitution of Eq. (
87
) and Eq. (
83
) into the replicated partition function in Eq. (
262
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}=\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}}\,\left\langle\exp\left[-\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}({\bm{w}}^{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{\alpha,\,\mu}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(w^{\mu}_{i}y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}-\sum_{\alpha,\,i}\left[\beta E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a)-\ell\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)\right]\right]\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\,,

(263)

where we used the shorthanded notation
\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}}\equiv\int\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\text{d}\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}
to denote an integral over the replicated space of soft-spin systems. The second term in the exponential comes from the fact that the connectivity matrix in the Wishart planted ensemble is defined to be traceless. We use Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation in Eq. (
125
) to linearize the term
({\bm{w}}^{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\alpha})^{2}
while introducing a set of
n\times M
continuous degrees of freedom
\{\phi^{\alpha}_{\mu}\}
for
\alpha=1,\ldots,\,n
and
\mu=1,\ldots,\,M
. We obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell})^{n}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\Biggl\langle\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha},\,\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}\exp\Biggl[-\sum_{\mu\alpha}\frac{(\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha})^{2}}{2}-i\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{N}}\sum_{\mu\alpha}\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}({\bm{w}}^{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\alpha})\\
&+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{\alpha,\,\mu,\,i}(w_{i}^{\mu}y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}-\sum_{\alpha,\,i}\left[\beta E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a)-\ell\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)\right]\Biggr]\Biggr\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}\,,\end{split}

(264)

where we employed a shorthanded notation for the integral
\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha},\,\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}\equiv\int\prod_{\alpha}\frac{\text{d}\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}\text{d}\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{N}}}
. The patterns
{\bm{w}}^{\mu}
are constructed from the realizations of the quenched disorder
{\bm{z}}^{\mu}
, as described in Eq. (
84
) of the main text. Writing explicitly the average over the quenched disorder we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell})^{n}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\int\prod_{\mu=1}^{M}\frac{\text{d}\mathbf{z}^{\mu}}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{N}}\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha},\,\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu,\alpha}(\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha})^{2}-\sum_{\alpha,i}\left[\beta E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a)-\ell\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)\right]\right]\\
&\times\prod_{\mu=1}^{M}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}z_{i}^{\mu}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{\beta}{N}\left(\sum_{k,\alpha}\Sigma^{1/2}_{ik}\Sigma_{jk}^{1/2}(y_{k}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)\right)z_{j}^{\mu}-i\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{N}}\sum_{\alpha}\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}\cdot\bm{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{z}^{\mu}\right]\,,\end{split}

(265)

where we see that the traceless property of the connectivity matrix
\text{\bf J}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
has the effect of changing the correlations of the quenched disorder. The inverse of the covariance matrix of the quenched variables
\{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}\}
is now given by the matrix

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{ij}&-\frac{\beta}{N}\sum_{k,\alpha}\Sigma_{ik}^{1/2}\Sigma_{jk}^{1/2}(y_{k}^{\alpha})^{2}=\delta_{ij}-\frac{\beta}{N-1}\sum_{\alpha,k}\left(\delta_{ik}-\frac{1}{N}\right)\left(\delta_{jk}-\frac{1}{N}\right)(y_{k}^{\alpha})^{2}\\
&=\delta_{ij}-\frac{\beta}{N-1}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\delta_{ij}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}-\frac{1}{N}\left((y_{j}^{\alpha})^{2}+(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{k}(y_{k}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)\\
&\approx\delta_{ij}\left(1-\frac{\beta}{N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(y^{\alpha}_{i})^{2}\right)\,,\end{split}

(266)

where in the last passage we have neglected all the terms of order
O(N^{-2})
or higher. By defining the variable
\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}
as

\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{N}}\sum_{\alpha}\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}\bm{\Sigma}^{1/2}\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}

(267)

we evaluate the Gaussian integral over the quenched disorder
{\bm{z}}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
264
), and we expand the argument of the exponential to the leading order in powers of
N^{-1}
, obtaining

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prod_{\mu=1}^{M}\int\frac{\text{d}{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\,&\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(z_{i}^{\mu})^{2}\left[1-\frac{\beta}{N}\sum_{\alpha}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}\right]-i\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\cdot{\bm{z}}^{\mu}\right]\\
&=\exp\left[-\frac{M}{2}\sum_{i}\log\left(1-\frac{\beta}{N}\sum_{\alpha}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}\right)\right]\prod_{\mu}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\frac{(\Gamma_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\frac{\beta}{N}\sum_{\alpha}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}}\right]\\
&\approx\exp\left[\frac{M\beta}{2N}\sum_{\alpha,i}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\cdot\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\right]\\
&=\exp\left[\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}\sum_{\alpha,i}(y_{i}^{\alpha})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\cdot\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}\right]\,.\end{split}

(268)

From this expression, we observe that the lack of a diagonal element in
\mathbf{J}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
contributes to a shift in the effective laser gain by an amount
\alpha=M/N
, the fraction of patterns stored in the connectivity matrix. Plugging Eq. (
268
) into Eq. (
265
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha},\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}\exp\left[{-\sum_{\mu\alpha}\frac{(\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha})^{2}}{2}-\beta\sum_{\alpha,i}E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a+\alpha)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\mu}(\Gamma_{i}^{\mu})^{2}+\ell\sum_{\alpha,i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)}\right]\\
&=\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha},\bm{\phi}^{\alpha}}\left(\prod_{\mu}\exp\left[{-\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{2}(\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha})^{2}-\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left(\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}\phi_{\mu}^{\beta}\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{y^{\beta}}-\frac{\phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}\phi_{\mu}^{\beta}}{N}(\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{t})(\mathbf{y}^{\beta}\cdot\mathbf{t})\right)}\right]\right)\\
&\times\text{e}^{-\beta\sum_{i\alpha}E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha,i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)}\,,\end{split}

(269)

where in the second line we have used the definition of
\bm{\Gamma}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
267
). Integrating out the Gaussian variables
\phi^{\alpha}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\text{\bf y}^{\alpha}}\exp\left[-\frac{M}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}_{n}+\beta\mathbf{K})-\beta\sum_{\alpha,i}E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha,i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)\right],

(270)

where the matrix
\mathbf{1}_{n}
and the matrix
K
are
n\times n
matrices. The former is the identity matrix in replica space, while the latter is defined as

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\beta}-\frac{1}{N^{2}}(\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{t})(\mathbf{y}^{\beta}\cdot\mathbf{t})\,.

(271)

Now we use the identity

\displaystyle f(a)=\int\text{d}x\,\delta(x-a)f(a)=\int\text{d}x\,\delta(x-a)f(x).

(272)

to insert a set of order parameters into the replicated partition function
\langle\left(Z_{\ell}\right)^{n}\rangle_{{\bm{z}}_{\mu}}
. The first one is an
n\times n
overlap matrix
\mathbf{Q}
, while the second one is a
n
-dimensional magnetization vector
{\bm{m}}
. We thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\int\left(\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\right)\int\left(\prod_{\alpha\leq\beta}\text{d}Q_{\alpha\beta}\right)\delta\left(Q_{\alpha\beta}-N^{-1}\sum_{i}y_{i}^{\alpha}y_{i}^{\beta}\right)\left(\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}m_{\alpha}\right)\delta\left(m_{\alpha}-N^{-1}\sum_{i}t_{i}y_{i}^{\alpha}\right)\\
&\times\exp\left[-\frac{M}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}+\beta\mathbf{K})\right]\text{e}^{-\beta\sum_{i\alpha}E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha,i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)}.\end{split}

(273)

Using the Fourier representation of the Delta distribution, we now introduce a set of Lagrange multipliers,
r_{\alpha\beta}
and
\widehat{m}_{\alpha}
, for each entries of
\mathbf{Q}
and
{\bm{m}}
, respectively.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}\mathbf{y}^{\alpha}\prod_{\alpha\leq\beta}\frac{i\text{d}r_{\alpha\beta}}{2\pi}\prod_{\alpha\leq\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}m_{\alpha}\prod_{\alpha}\frac{i\text{d}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}}{2\pi}\text{e}^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha,i}E_{I}(y_{i}^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha,i}\delta(y_{i}^{\alpha}-x)}\\
&\times\exp\left[-\frac{M}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}+\beta\mathbf{Q}-\beta{\bm{m}}\otimes{\bm{m}})-\sum_{\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{2}r_{\alpha\beta}(NQ_{\alpha\beta}-\sum_{i}y_{i}^{\alpha}y_{i}^{\beta})-\sum_{\alpha}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}(Nm_{\alpha}-\sum_{i}t_{i}y_{i}^{\alpha})\right]\end{split}

(274)

After rescaling
r_{\alpha\beta}\to\alpha\beta^{2}r_{\alpha\beta}
,
\widehat{m}_{\gamma}\to\alpha\beta\widehat{m}_{\gamma}
, and performing more steps of algebra, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\left(Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\ell}\right)^{n}\right\rangle_{{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}&=\int\text{d}\mathbf{\Gamma}\exp N\Biggl[-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}+\beta\mathbf{Q}-\beta{\bm{m}}\otimes{\bm{m}})-\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}r_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}-\alpha\beta\sum_{\alpha}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}+\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\Biggr]\Biggr\rvert_{\ell=0}\\
&\equiv\int\text{d}\bm{\Gamma}\,\text{e}^{NS^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}[\bm{\Gamma}]}\,,\end{split}

(275)

where we denoted by
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{Q},{\bm{m}},\hat{{\bm{m}}}\}
the set of various order parameters, and by
\text{d}\bm{\Gamma}
the product of their volume factors in phase space. We also defined an action
S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\bm{\Gamma})
and a mean field partition function
Z^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\,\ell}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}[\mathbf{\Gamma}]&=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}+\beta\mathbf{Q}-\beta{\bm{m}}\otimes{\bm{m}})-\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}r_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}-\alpha\beta\sum_{\alpha}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}+\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\,,\\
Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}&=\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}y^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}r_{\alpha\beta}y^{\alpha}y^{\beta}+\alpha\beta\sum_{\alpha}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}-\beta\sum_{\alpha}E_{I}(y^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha}\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)}\,.\end{split}

(276)

Substituting Eq. (
275
) into Eq. (
261
) and taking the limit
N\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\delta(x_{i}-x)\rangle_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\ell}\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\log\int\text{d}\mathbf{\Gamma}\,\text{e}^{NS^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}[\mathbf{\Gamma}]}.

(277)

As in Appendix
A
, we ignore the subleading normalization factors in
\text{d}\mathbf{\Gamma}
. In the limit
N\to\infty
, the integral on the right hand side of Eq. (
277
) is evaluated at the saddle point of the action
S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\bm{\Gamma})
. The saddle point equations are given in Eq. (
103
) of the main text. After the saddle point evaluation, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\delta(x_{i}-x)\rangle_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}&=\partial_{\ell}\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\Biggl[-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{Tr}\log(\mathbf{1}+\beta\mathbf{Q}-\beta{\bm{m}}\otimes{\bm{m}})\\
&-\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}r_{\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}-\alpha\beta\sum_{\alpha}\widehat{m}_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}+\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\Biggr]\Biggr|_{\ell=0}.\end{split}

(278)

The derivative over
\ell
eliminates all but the last term on the right hand side of the above equation, thus yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\delta(x_{i}-x)\rangle_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}^{\mu}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}y^{\alpha}\left(\sum_{\alpha}\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)\right)\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(\{y^{\alpha}\})},

(279)

where the evaluation at
\ell=0
has been performed. This is Eq. (
99
) in the main text. In the next Section, we evaluate the expression above using the replica-symmetric ansatz.

I.1
Replica-symmetric ansatz

In the replica-symmetric phase, we take
Q_{\alpha\beta}=q_{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}+q_{o}(1-\delta_{\alpha\beta}),\ r_{\alpha\beta}=r_{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}+r_{o}(1-\delta_{\alpha\beta})
. We take
m_{\alpha}=m,\widehat{m}_{\alpha}=\widehat{m}
. The logarithm of the modified mean field partition function
\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\,\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}},\,\ell}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}&=\log\int\prod_{\alpha}\text{d}y^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}r_{o}\left(\sum_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}\right)^{2}+\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}(r_{d}-r_{o})\sum_{\alpha}(y^{\alpha})^{2}+\alpha\beta\widehat{m}\sum_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}-\beta\sum_{\alpha}E_{I}(y^{\alpha},a+\alpha)+\ell\sum_{\alpha}\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)}\\
&=\log\overline{\prod_{\alpha}\left[\int\text{d}y^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{-\beta\sqrt{\alpha r_{o}}hy^{\alpha}+\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}(r_{d}-r_{o})(y^{\alpha})^{2}+\alpha\beta\widehat{m}y^{\alpha}-\beta E_{I}(y^{a},a+\alpha)+\ell\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)}\right]}\\
&=\log\overline{\left[\int\text{d}y^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{-\beta\sqrt{\alpha r_{o}}hy^{\alpha}+\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}(r_{d}-r_{o})(y^{\alpha})^{2}+\alpha\beta\widehat{m}y^{\alpha}-\beta E_{I}(y^{a},a+\alpha)+\ell\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)}\right]^{n}}\\
&=n\,\overline{\log\left[\int\text{d}y^{\alpha}\,\text{e}^{-\beta\sqrt{\alpha r_{o}}hy^{\alpha}+\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}(r_{d}-r_{o})(y^{\alpha})^{2}+\alpha\beta\widehat{m}y^{\alpha}-\beta E_{I}(y^{a},a+\alpha)+\ell\delta(y^{\alpha}-x)}\right]}+O(n)\\
&\equiv n\,\overline{\log\int\text{d}y\,\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)+\ell\delta(y-x)}}+O(n)\,.\end{split}

(280)

In the second line, we performed a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation to linearize the squared sum over the replicated spins
y^{\alpha}
, thus introducing a Gaussian random field
h
of mean zero and unit variance. The overline denotes an average over this Gaussian distribution. In the third line, we used the fact that integral over the different replicas factorizes into the product of
n
identical integrals. In the fourth line, we expanded to the lowest order in
n
. The last line defined an effective single-site mean field energy
E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)
, which is given in Eq. (
107
) of the main text. Through these manipulations, Eq. (
279
) in the replica-symmetric phase becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}P^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}(x)&=\overline{\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)}[Z_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}]^{-1}}\\
&\equiv P_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x)\,,\end{split}

(281)

which is Eq. (
106
) in the main text. Following relatively similar manipulations to the ones performed above and in Appendices
A
and
C
, we obtain the saddle point equations ofEq. (
103
) in the main text.

I.2
Stability of the replica symmetric solution

To investigate the stability of the replica-symmetric phase, we study the Hessian of the action
S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
with respect to perturbations in the space of overlaps
Q_{\alpha\beta}
and Lagrange multipliers
r_{\alpha\beta}
.The Hessian matrix has the following block structure,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\gamma\delta}}&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}&\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}\end{bmatrix}\,.\end{split}

(282)

Let us observe first that the top right and bottom left blocks are diagonal, because of the term proportional to
Q_{\alpha\beta}r_{\alpha\beta}
in the action
S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
in Eq. (
276
). The structure of the Hessian matrix is then identical to the one that arises when studying the stability of the replica symmetric phase in the Hopfield or anti-Hopfield models
Amit et al. 1987
;
Nokura 1998
. The condition for the stability of the convex phase derived in those cases applies also here, and it reads

1-\frac{1}{\alpha\beta^{2}}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}}-2\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\alpha\gamma}}+\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\gamma\delta}}\right]\left[\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\alpha\beta}}-2\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\alpha\gamma}}+\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}\right]<0.

(283)

We compute first the terms involving derivatives with respect to
Q_{\alpha\beta}
, which depend only on the logarithmic term in the action
S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
in Eq. (
276
). To derive this, we first use the following identity, valid for any continuous function
f
and any symmetric matrix
A
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{A_{\alpha\beta}}\Tr f(\text{\bf A})&=f^{\prime}(\text{\bf A})_{\alpha\beta}+f^{\prime}(\text{\bf A})_{\beta\alpha}\\
&-\delta_{\alpha\beta}f^{\prime}(\text{\bf A})_{\alpha\beta}\,.\end{split}

(284)

In our case,
f(\text{\bf A})=\log(\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B})
. The first derivative of
f
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{\prime}(\text{\bf A})_{\alpha\beta}&=(\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B})^{-1}_{\alpha\beta}+(\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B})^{-1}_{\beta\alpha}\\
&-\delta_{\alpha\beta}(\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B})^{-1}_{\alpha\beta}.\end{split}

(285)

Now we let
\text{\bf K}\equiv(\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B})^{-1}
. We seek
\partial_{A_{\gamma\delta}}K_{\alpha\beta}
. Using a matrix calculus identity, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{A_{\gamma\delta}}K_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\rho\sigma}K_{\alpha\rho}\frac{\partial A_{\rho\sigma}}{\partial A_{\gamma\delta}}K_{\sigma\beta}

(286)

Because
A
is symmetric,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial A_{\rho\sigma}}{\partial A_{\gamma\delta}}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\gamma}\mathbb{I}_{\sigma\delta}+\mathbb{I}_{\rho\delta}\mathbb{I}_{\sigma\gamma},\ \gamma\neq\delta\\
\mathbb{I}_{\rho\gamma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\gamma},\ \gamma=\delta\,,\end{cases}

(287)

where we have used the symbol
\mathbb{I}
to represent an indicator function (i.e. a Kronecker delta) to avoid confusion with the
\delta
representing an index.

We thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial^{2}\Tr f(A)}{\partial A_{\alpha\beta}\partial A_{\gamma\delta}}=\begin{cases}2\partial_{A_{\gamma\delta}}K_{\alpha\beta},\ \alpha\neq\beta\\
\partial_{A_{\gamma\delta}}K_{\alpha\alpha},\ \alpha=\beta\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}-2(K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\delta\beta}+K_{\alpha\delta}K_{\beta\gamma}),\ \alpha\neq\beta,\gamma\neq\delta\\
-2K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\gamma\beta},\ \alpha\neq\beta,\gamma=\delta\\
-(K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\delta\alpha}+K_{\alpha\delta}K_{\gamma\alpha}),\ \alpha=\beta,\gamma\neq\delta\\
-K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\alpha\gamma},\ \alpha=\beta,\gamma=\delta.\end{cases}\end{split}

(288)

In our case,
\text{\bf A}+\text{\bf B}=\mathbf{1}_{n}+\beta\mathbf{Q}-\beta{\bm{m}}\otimes{\bm{m}}
. Therefore in the replica symmetric phase,
K
becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=\left((1+\Delta\widetilde{q})\mathbf{1}_{n}+\beta(q_{o}-m^{2}){\bm{t}}_{n}\otimes{\bm{t}}_{n}\right)^{-1}\,,

(289)

where
{\bm{t}}_{n}\equiv[1,\ldots,1]^{\mathrm{T}}
is a
n
-dimensional vector with all entries equal to
1
. Using the Sherman-Morrison formula
Bartlett 1951
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{K}&=\frac{1}{1+\Delta\widetilde{q}}\mathbf{1}_{n}-\frac{\beta(q_{o}-m^{2})}{(1+\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}{\bm{t}}_{n}\otimes{\bm{t}}_{n}\\
&=-\Delta\widetilde{r}\mathbf{1}_{n}-\beta r_{o}{\bm{t}}_{n}\otimes{\bm{t}}_{n}\,,\end{split}

(290)

where in the second line we used the replica-symmetric saddle point equations given by Eq. (
108
).
We can now compute all the matrix elements of the top left block of the Hessian of the CIM-WPE action in Eq. (
282
), obtaining,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\alpha}\partial Q_{\alpha\alpha}}&=\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}K_{\alpha\alpha}^{2}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\alpha}\partial Q_{\alpha\gamma}}&=\alpha\beta^{2}K_{\alpha\alpha}K_{\gamma\alpha}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\alpha}Q_{\gamma\delta}}&=\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}\left(K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\delta\alpha}+K_{\alpha\delta}K_{\gamma\alpha}\right)\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\alpha}\partial Q_{\gamma\gamma}}&=\frac{\alpha\beta^{2}}{2}K_{\alpha\gamma}^{2}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}}&=\alpha\beta^{2}(K_{\alpha\alpha}^{2}+K_{\alpha\beta}^{2})\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\alpha\gamma}}&=\alpha\beta^{2}(K_{\alpha\alpha}K_{\beta\gamma}+K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\alpha\beta})\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha\beta}\partial Q_{\gamma\delta}}&=\alpha\beta^{2}(K_{\alpha\gamma}K_{\delta\beta}+K_{\alpha\delta}K_{\beta\gamma})\end{split}

(291)

The elements of
K
are, in the replica symmetric phase,

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{\alpha\alpha}&=\frac{1}{1+\Delta\widetilde{q}}-\frac{\beta(q_{o}-m^{2})}{(1+\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}\\
&=-\Delta\widetilde{r}-\beta r_{o}\\
K_{\alpha\beta}&=-\frac{\beta(q_{o}-m^{2})}{(1+\Delta\widetilde{q})^{2}}\\
&=-\beta r_{o}\,,\end{split}

(292)

with
\alpha\neq\beta
.

Now we turn to the computation of derivatives with respect to the Lagrange multipliers
r_{\alpha\beta}
, which involve the term
-\log Z_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
in Eq. (
276
). Using the fact that
r_{\alpha\beta}
is a symmetric matrix, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\partial_{r_{\alpha\beta}}\partial_{r_{\gamma\delta}}\log Z&=-\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}x^{\gamma}x^{\delta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\\
&+\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\langle x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\langle x^{\gamma}x^{\delta}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{MF}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\,.\end{split}

(293)

In the replica-symmetric phase, the different types of matrix elements become

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\alpha}\partial r_{\alpha\alpha}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\langle x^{4}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}-\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}^{2}\right]\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\alpha}\partial r_{\alpha\gamma}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\langle x^{3}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}-\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}\,\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}\right]\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\alpha}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\Bigl[\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}\\
&-\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}\,\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}\Bigr]=0\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\alpha}\partial r_{\gamma\gamma}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\left(\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}^{2}\right]=0\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\alpha\beta}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\left(\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}^{2}\right]\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\alpha\gamma}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\langle x^{2}\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}^{2}\right]\\
\frac{\partial^{2}S^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}}{\partial r_{\alpha\beta}\partial r_{\gamma\delta}}&=\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}\left[\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{4}}-\overline{\left(\langle x\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right)^{2}}^{2}\right]=0\,,\end{split}

(294)

where
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}
is an average over the Boltzmann distribution of the effective mean field energy
E^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}(x,h)
, for a given realization of the quenched field
h
, namely,

\langle\ldots\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\equiv\frac{\int\text{d}x\,\ldots\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)}}{\int\text{d}x\,\text{e}^{-\beta E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)}}\,,

(295)

and we recall that the overline denotes an average over the realizations of the quenched field
h
, which follows a Gaussian distribution of zero mean and unit variance. Plugging the relevant entries of Eq. (
291
) and Eq. (
294
) into the stability condition in Eq. (
283
), and evalutaing the resulting expression in the low temperature limit
\beta\to\infty
, we obtain the stability condition

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\text{\tiny{R}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}&\equiv-1+\alpha\Delta\widetilde{r}^{2}\beta^{2}\overline{\left[\left\langle x^{2}-\left(\langle x\rangle^{\text{\tiny WPE}}_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle_{\text{\tiny{RS}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}\right]^{2}}\\
&=-1+\alpha\Delta\widetilde{r}^{2}\overline{\left[\partial^{2}_{x}E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)\rvert_{x=x^{*}(h)}\right]^{-2}}<0\,,\end{split}

(296)

where
x^{*}(h)
is the global minimum of the effective energy
E_{\text{\tiny{eff}}}^{\text{\tiny{WPE}}}(x,h)
for a fixed realization of
h
. This is the stability condition given by Eq. (
109
) of the main text.
